Supplemental Appendix to:
Order-Explicit Linearization of High-Dimensional

Abstract
We give an order-explicit large deviation bound for the difference between a high-dimensional U U -statistic and its Hájek projection. In particular, we show that any U U -statistic of order b b on n n observations, with a d d -dimensional kernel whose coordinates have ψ 1 \psi_{1} -Orlicz norm at most ϕ \phi , has a maximum deviation from its Hájek projection of order O p ​ ( ϕ ​ b ​ n − 1 ​ log 2 ⁡ ( d ​ n ) ) O_{p}(\phi bn^{-1}\log^{2}(dn)) . The proof relies on the development of novel order-explicit moment inequalities for higher-order Hoeffding components. We show that this rate is unimprovable, up to the polynomial factor on the logarithmic term. As corollaries, we obtain new Bernstein-type concentration and Gaussian approximation results for high-dimensional U U -statistics. We apply these results to establish the consistency of a set of resampling-based simultaneous confidence intervals built around a class of nonparametric regression estimators constructed with subsampled kernels. This class encompasses several forms of random forest regression, including Generalized Random Forests. Keywords: U U -statistics, Random Forest Regression, Half-Sample Bootstrap



1.
Introduction

Let
\mathbf{D}_{n}=(D_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
collect independent observations from a distribution
P
on a set
\mathcal{D}
. For each symmetric,
d
-dimensional kernel function
u:\mathcal{D}^{b}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, define the
b
-order
U
-statistic

(1.1)

\displaystyle U_{n,b}(u)={n\choose b}^{-1}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}u(D_{\mathsf{s}})\penalty\ ,

where the set
\mathcal{S}_{n,b}
collects all of the subsets of
[n]=\{1,\ldots,n\}
of size
b
and the quantity
D_{\mathsf{s}}
collects the subset of the observed data
\mathbf{D}_{n}
with indices in the set
\mathsf{s}
.

The canonical approach for studying the large-sample behavior of statistics with this structure, due to
Hoeffding, (1948)
, is based on the observation that the projection of the average (
1.1
) onto the space of statistics that decompose additively into the contributions of individual observations takes the form

(1.2)

\displaystyle\frac{b}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}u^{(1)}(D_{i})\penalty\ ,\quad\text{where}\quad u^{(1)}(D)=\mathbb{E}[u(D_{[b]})\mid D_{1}=D]-\mathbb{E}[u(D_{[b]})]\penalty\ .

The average (
1.2
) is known as the Hájek projection of the
U
-statistic (
1.1
), following
Hájek, (1968)
. The essential idea is that, if the residual from this projection can be shown to be of a lower stochastic order, then the problem reduces to the analysis of an average of i.i.d. random variables. In particular, under classical regularity conditions,
Hoeffding, (1948)
establishes that if the dimension
d=1
and the order
b
is fixed, then

(1.3)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{n}{\sigma^{2}_{b}b^{2}}}(U_{n,b}(u)-\mathbb{E}[u(D_{[b]})])\overset{d}{\to}\mathsf{N}(0,1)\penalty\ ,\quad\text{where}\quad\sigma^{2}_{b}=\Var(u^{(1)}(D_{i}))\penalty\ .

See
Serfling, (1980)
,
Lee, (1990)
, and
Van der Vaart, (2000)
for textbook treatments.

In this paper, we extend this approach to settings where the order
b
and the dimension
d
are non-negligible relative to the sample size
n
. Formally, we obtain the following order-explicit large deviation bound on the residual from the Hájek projection. Throughout, the quantities
c
and
C
denote universal positive constants, whose values are allowed to change in each appearance. We write
A\lesssim B
if
A\leq CB
.

Theorem 1.1
.

Consider a symmetric kernel function
u:\mathcal{D}^{b}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
. If the order
b
satisfies
b\log(dn)\leq cn
and each component
u_{j}(\cdot)
of
u(\cdot)
satisfies

(1.4)

\displaystyle\|u_{j}(D_{[b]})\|_{\psi_{1}}\leq\phi\penalty\ ,

then the large deviation bound

(1.5)

\displaystyle\bigg\|U_{n,b}(u)-\mathbb{E}[u(D_{[b]})]-\frac{b}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}u^{(1)}(D_{i})\bigg\|_{\infty}\lesssim\frac{b}{n}\phi\log^{2}(dn)

holds with probability greater than
1-C/nd
.

We give the proof in
Section

2
. Moreover, we show that the bound (
1.5
) is unimprovable up to the polynomial factor on the logarithmic term.

Theorem

1.1
generates two corollaries through the application of the idea developed in
Hoeffding, (1948)
, each closing an open problem in the large-sample approximation of high-dimensional
U
-statistics. The first corollary is the following order-explicit Bernstein-type large deviation bound. The proof follows by combining
Theorem

1.1
with a standard Bernstein inequality for i.i.d. sums, and is deferred to
Appendix

A
.

Corollary 1.1
.

Define the Hájek projection variance
\sigma^{2}_{b,j}=\Var(u^{(1)}_{j}(D_{i}))
and set
\overline{\sigma}_{b}=\max_{j\in[d]}\sigma_{b,j}
. If the conditions of
Theorem

1.1
hold, then the inequality

(1.6)

\Big\|U_{n,b}(u)-\mathbb{E}[u(D_{[b]})]\Big\|_{\infty}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{b^{2}\overline{\sigma}^{2}_{b}\log(nd)}{n}}+\phi\frac{b}{n}\log^{2}(nd)

holds with probability greater than
1-C/nd
.

Large deviation bounds for
U
-statistics were first given in
Hoeffding, (1963)
(see Lemma A.5 of
Song et al., (2019)
for a modern statement). However, the leading term in the bound given there takes the form

(1.7)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{b\overline{\nu}\log(dn)}{n}}\penalty\ ,\quad\text{where}\quad\overline{\nu}=\max_{j\in[d]}\nu_{j}\quad\text{and}\quad\nu_{j}=\Var(u_{j}(D_{[b]}))\penalty\ .

As the bound
b\sigma^{2}_{b,j}\leq\nu_{j}
follows from Hoeffding orthogonality, the quantity (
1.7
) is larger than the leading term in (
1.6
).
1
1

1

In part motivated by the deficiency of the
Hoeffding, (1963)
bound,
Arcones and Giné, (1993)
,
Arcones, (1995)
,
Giné et al., (2000)
,
Adamczak, (2008)
, and
Peel et al., (2010)
establish a series of refined large deviation bounds for high-dimensional
U
-statistics that use appropriate normalizing factors (among many other related results). However, the constants used to express these bounds depend implicitly on the order
b
.
Maurer, (2019)
and
Minsker, (2023)
give large deviation bounds for
U
-statistics that can converge when the order is increasing, but place much stronger assumptions on the regularity and smoothness of the kernel under consideration.
Seen differently, through comparison to the asymptotic approximation (
1.3
), the leading term of the non-asymptotic bound (
1.6
) uses the correct normalizing quantity.

The difference between the leading term in the bound (
1.6
) and the quantity (
1.7
) can be large. For example,
Song et al., (2019)
consider a non-parametric regression estimator based on a random forest construction that can be represented as a
b
-order
U
-statistic with
\sigma^{2}_{b,j}\asymp b^{-2}
and
\nu_{j}\asymp 1
. In this case, the ratio
b\overline{\sigma}^{2}_{b}/\overline{\nu}
is proportional to
b^{-1}
and so the leading term in the bound (
1.6
) is negligible relative to the quantity (
1.7
) in regimes where
b
is increasing. In more extreme, although not uncommon, cases the Hájek projection variance can be exactly zero, giving rise to a degenerate
U
-statistic. See, for instance, the test statistics considered in
Gretton et al., (2012)
and
Shekhar et al., (2023)
. In these settings, the bound (
1.6
) improves on the
Hoeffding, (1963)
bound by a factor of
(b/n)^{1/2}
, up to logarithmic terms.

The second corollary is the following order-explicit central limit theorem for high-dimensional
U
-statistics. The proof follows by combining
Theorem

1.1
with the high-dimensional central limit theorem for i.i.d. sums stated in
Chernozhukov et al., (2022)
, and is deferred to
Appendix

A
.
2
2

2

The boundedness condition (
1.8
) can be relaxed to the bound
\mathbb{E}[(u_{j}^{(1)}(D_{i}))^{4}]\leq\phi^{2}\sigma_{b,j}^{2}
for each
j
.

Corollary 1.2
.

Continue the notation introduced in
Theorem

1.1
. Define
\underline{\sigma}_{b}=\min_{j\in[d]}\sigma_{b,j}
. If the order
b
satisfies
b\log(dn)\leq cn
and the kernel
u(\cdot)
satisfies the bound

(1.8)

\displaystyle\|u(D_{[b]})-\mathbb{E}[u(D_{[b]})]\|_{\infty}\leq\phi

almost surely, then the inequality

(1.9)

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathsf{R}\in\mathcal{R}}\bigg|P\left\{\sqrt{\frac{n}{b^{2}}}\Sigma_{b}^{-1/2}(U_{n,b}-\mathbb{E}[u(D_{[b]})])\in\mathsf{R}\right\}-P\left\{\Sigma_{b}^{-1/2}Z\in\mathsf{R}\right\}\bigg|\lesssim\left(\frac{\phi^{2}\log^{5}(dn)}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}_{b}n}\right)^{1/4}

holds, where
\Sigma_{b}=\mathsf{Diag}(\sigma^{2}_{b,1},\ldots,\sigma^{2}_{b,d})
,
Z
denotes a centered Gaussian vector in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with the same covariance matrix as
u^{(1)}(D_{i})
, and
\mathcal{R}
denotes the set of hyper-rectangles in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

The
Hoeffding, (1948)
univariate central limit (
1.3
) was extended only recently to the regime where
b
is non-negligible relative to
n
. Under the condition that
b^{-1}\lesssim\sigma_{b}^{2}
,
DiCiccio and Romano, (2022)
give a result that applies to the regime
b=o(n^{1/2})
.
Wager and Athey, (2018)
and
Peng et al., (2022)
strengthen this result to the full regime
b=o(n)
. The results developed in each of these papers apply only to the case that
d=1
. In parallel,
Chen, (2018)
,
Chen and Kato, (2019)
, and
Song et al., (2019)
give results that apply to the case where
d
can be large relative to
n
. Of these papers, only
Song et al., (2019)
gives results with explicit dependence on the order
b
. However, their results are only applicable to the regime
b^{2}\underline{\sigma}_{b}^{-2}\log^{7}(dn)=o(n)
. Thus, in settings where
b^{-1}\lesssim\underline{\sigma}^{2}_{b}
, this result applies to the regime
b^{3}\log^{7}(dn)=o(n)
. By contrast, in this case, the central limit theorem (
1.9
) applies to the full regime
b\log^{5}(dn)=o(n)
.

This refinement can be essential for the analysis of modern statistical methods that aggregate algorithms computed on subsamples of a larger data set. To illustrate this point, in
Section

3
, we apply
Corollary

1.2
to establish the consistency of an approach for constructing resampling-based simultaneous confidence intervals around nonparametric regression estimators based on subsampled kernels, including subsampled nearest-neighbor regression

(
Fix and Hodges,, 1989
;
Khosravi et al.,, 2019
;
Demirkaya et al.,, 2024
)
and random forest regression

(
Breiman,, 2001
;
Mentch and Hooker,, 2016
;
Wager and Athey,, 2018
)
.

Our analysis again follows the agenda set out by
Hoeffding, (1948)
, and makes essential use of
Corollary

1.2
. We first show that, under regularity conditions, the estimators under consideration are well-approximated by
U
-statistics of order
b
with
d
-dimensional kernels that satisfy the bound
b^{-1}\lesssim\sigma_{b}^{2}
. However, these estimators are only consistent if the subsample size
b
satisfies
n^{p/(p+c)}=o(b)
, where
p
is the dimension of the available covariates. This places the relevant regime outside of the range
b=o(n^{1/3})
covered by available high-dimensional central limit theorems, while scalar results that allow for larger values of
b
cannot account for growth in the kernel dimension
d
.
Corollary

1.2
closes this gap by showing that high-dimensional Gaussian approximation holds at the subsample sizes required for consistency.

Our treatment of this application builds on
Oprescu et al., (2019)
, who augment the Generalized Random Forests framework proposed by
Athey et al., (2019)
to study pointwise inference for subsampled random forest estimators with nuisance parameters. Our results are comparable to the bounds on the accuracy of Gaussian multiplier bootstrap confidence regions for nonparametric regression and
Z
-estimation given in
Chernozhukov et al., (2014)
and
Belloni et al., (2018)
. We generalize these results, in the sense that we treat inference for conditional
Z
-estimators whose score function is potentially unknown to the researcher.

The improvement of
Theorem

1.1
over existing results is the consequence of a set of new order-explicit moment inequalities for higher-order components of Hoeffding decompositions. In the scalar case, existing arguments rely on pairing the total orthogonality of the Hoeffding decomposition with Chebyshev-type bounds. This approach is insufficient for the high-dimensional case, which requires exponential tail bounds. Existing high-dimensional results instead build on the decoupling and symmetrization arguments surveyed in
De la Pena and Giné, (1999)
. However, available decoupling bounds incur poor dependence on the order
b
, which becomes prohibitive when
b
is non-negligible.

Our main technical innovation is a new order-explicit one-sided decoupling bound for
U
-statistics that is sufficient to resolve this tension. We show that the quantity that results from this bound can be controlled by combining a symmetrization argument due to
Sherman, (1994)
with a specialization of a moment inequality from
Giné et al., (2000)
. This approach yields exponential tail bounds for higher-order Hoeffding terms that have the correct scaling in the regime where both
b
and
d
are large.

Additional applications of large order
U
-statistics to split-sample testing, robust estimation, and kernel density estimation are reviewed in
DiCiccio and Romano, (2022)
,
Minsker, (2023)
, and
Song et al., (2019)
. Omitted proofs are given in
Appendices

A
,
B
, and
C
.
Appendices

D
,
E
, and
F
give additional results and discussion that will be introduced at appropriate points throughout the paper.

2.
Proof of
Theorem

1.1

In this section, we outline the proof of
Theorem

1.1
. Throughout the proof and supporting Lemmas, we maintain
\mathbb{E}[u(D_{[b]})]=0
without loss. The result is obtained in two steps. First, we decompose the difference between the
U
-statistic under consideration and its Hájek projection through a Hoeffding expansion
(
Hoeffding,, 1948
;
Efron and Stein,, 1981
)
. We adopt the following formulation of Hoeffding’s expansion based on the use of stochastic differences, stated as Part (i) of the following Lemma (see e.g.,
Lachièze-Rey and Peccati, (2017)
). Part (ii) gives the well-known specialization of this result to
U
-statistics (see e.g., Lemma 5.1.5 A of
Serfling, (1980)
).

Lemma 2.1

(Hoeffding’s Expansion)
.

Fix a deterministic function
f:\mathcal{D}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
.

(i)
Let
D^{\prime}_{[m]}
denote an independent copy of
D_{[m]}
. Construct
D^{(i)}_{[m]}
by replacing
D_{i}
with
D_{i}^{\prime}
in
D_{[m]}
, leaving all other entries unchanged. For each index
i
, define the difference operator

(2.1)

\nabla_{i}f(D_{[m]})=f(D^{(i)}_{[m]})-f(D_{[m]})\penalty\ .

If the moment
\mathbb{E}[f(D_{[m]})^{2}]
is finite, then the representation

(2.2)

\displaystyle f(D_{[m]})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[f(D_{[m]})]+\sum_{l=1}^{m}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{m,l}}f^{(l)}(D_{\mathsf{s}})\penalty\ ,\quad\text{where}

\displaystyle f^{(l)}(D_{i_{1}},...,D_{i_{l}})

\displaystyle=(-1)^{l}\mathbb{E}[\nabla_{i_{1}}\cdots\nabla_{i_{l}}f(D_{[m]})\mid D_{i_{1}},...,D_{i_{l}}]\penalty\ ,

holds, the non-constant summands appearing in (
2.2
) are mean-zero, and all
2^{m}
summands are uncorrelated.

(ii)
For each integer
l\leq n
, define the operator

(2.3)

\displaystyle U_{n,l}(h)={n\choose l}^{-1}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,l}}h(D_{\mathsf{s}})

on the set of measurable functions
h:\mathcal{D}^{l}\to\mathbb{R}
. If the statistic
f(D_{[m]})
is mean-zero and has a finite variance and the function
f(\cdot)
is symmetric in its arguments, then the decomposition

(2.4)

\displaystyle U_{n,m}(f)=\sum_{l=1}^{m}{m\choose l}U_{n,l}(f^{(l)})

holds and all
m
summands appearing in (
2.4
) are uncorrelated.

In particular, specialized to our setting, we obtain the decomposition

(2.5)

\displaystyle U_{n,b}(u)-\frac{b}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}u^{(1)}(D_{i})

\displaystyle=\sum_{m=2}^{b}{b\choose m}U_{n,m}(u^{(m)})

by applying Part (ii) of
Lemma

2.1
.

Second, and more substantively, we bound each of the summands of the decomposition (
2.5
) by applying the following new higher-order moment bound.

Lemma 2.2
.

Fix an integer
1\leq m\leq\min\{b,n/2\}
. If the function
f:\mathcal{D}^{b}\to\mathbb{R}
is symmetric in its arguments and satisfies the bound

(2.6)

\displaystyle\|f(D_{[b]})\|_{\psi_{1}}\leq\phi\penalty\ ,

then the higher-order moment inequality

(2.7)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|{b\choose m}U_{n,m}(f^{(m)})\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq\phi q\left(\frac{Cqb}{n}\right)^{m/2}

holds for each
2\vee 2\log(n)\leq q\leq cn/b
.

The essential novelty of the bound is that the dependence on the order
m
is kept explicit. The proof, given in the following subsection, develops a new one-sided decoupling bound for
U
-statistics of an arbitrary order, which avoids introducing order-dependent constants present in previous work (see, for comparison,
De la Peña and Montgomery-Smith, (1995)
and Chapter 3.1 of
De la Pena and Giné, (1999)
). The argument then proceeds by combining symmetrization, Bonami’s inequality, and a Rosenthal-type decomposition due to
Giné et al., (2000)
to control the resulting quantity. The final bound is obtained through the application of a new estimate for higher-order moments of conditional expectations of Hoeffding components, derived from a further application of Hoeffding orthogonality.

To complete the proof, observe that by setting
q=2\vee 2\log(nd)
and the constant
c
sufficiently small, the assumption
b\log(nd)\leq cn
guarantees that
q\leq cn/b
and
b\leq n/2
, so that
Lemma

2.2
applies. Thus, for each integer
m\in\{2,\ldots,b\}
and each component
u_{j}(\cdot)
of the kernel
u(\cdot)
, Markov’s inequality yields

\displaystyle P\left\{\bigg|{b\choose m}U_{n,m}(u_{j}^{(m)})\bigg|\geq 2e\phi\log(nd)\left(\frac{Cb\log(nd)}{n}\right)^{m/2}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\exp(-2\log(nd))\frac{\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|{b\choose m}U_{n,m}(u_{j}^{(m)})\bigg|^{2\log(nd)}\right]}{\left(\phi\log(nd)\left(\frac{Cb\log(nd)}{n}\right)^{m/2}\right)^{2\log(nd)}}

(2.8)

\displaystyle\leq\exp(-2\log(nd))=\frac{1}{n^{2}d^{2}}\penalty\ .

Moreover, by taking the universal constant
c
sufficiently small, the condition
b\log(dn)\leq cn
again implies

(2.9)

\displaystyle\phi\log(dn)\sum_{m=2}^{b}\left(\frac{Cb\log(dn)}{n}\right)^{m/2}\lesssim\frac{\phi b\log^{2}(dn)}{n}\penalty\ .

Consequently, the decomposition (
2.5
) and a union bound imply that

\displaystyle P\left\{\bigg\|U_{n,b}(u)-\frac{b}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}u^{(1)}(D_{i})\bigg\|_{\infty}\geq C\frac{\phi b\log^{2}(dn)}{n}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{d}\sum_{m=2}^{b}P\left\{\bigg|{b\choose m}U_{n,m}(u_{j}^{(m)})\bigg|\geq 2e\phi\log(nd)\left(\frac{Cb\log(nd)}{n}\right)^{m/2}\right\}

(2.10)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{b-1}{n^{2}d}\leq\frac{C}{nd}\penalty\ ,

as required.

Remark 2.1
.

The bound (
2.10
) is unimprovable up to the polynomial factor on the logarithmic term. In particular, in
Appendix

A
we show that, for each
n\geq b\geq 2
and
d\geq 1
, there exists a symmetric kernel function
u:\mathcal{D}^{b}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
whose coordinates satisfy the bound (
1.4
) and such that the lower bound

(2.11)

\displaystyle P\left\{\bigg\|U_{n,b}(u)-\mathbb{E}[u(D_{[b]})]-\frac{b}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}u^{(1)}(D_{i})\bigg\|_{\infty}\geq c\frac{\phi b\log(nd)}{n}\right\}\geq\frac{c}{nd}\penalty\ .

holds. That is, the factor
\phi b/n
and at least one logarithmic factor in
nd
are unavoidable.
\blacksquare

2.1.
Proof of
Lemma

2.2

Classical bounds for moments of higher-order Hoeffding components are obtained from an instance of a decoupling and symmetrization argument. The motivating idea is that a
U
-statistic would be easier to analyze if each of the arguments of its kernel were computed on a separate, independent sample. To see this, let
\mathbf{D}_{n}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{D}_{n}^{(m)}
denote
m
independent copies of the data
\mathbf{D}_{n}
and consider the decoupled statistic

(2.12)

\displaystyle\widetilde{U}_{n,m}(f^{(m)})=\binom{n}{m}^{-1}\sum_{(i_{1},\ldots,i_{m})\in\mathcal{S}_{n,m}}f^{(m)}(D_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\ldots,D_{i_{m}}^{(m)})\penalty\ .

Panels A and B of
Figure

1
visualize the summands of the
statistics
U_{n,2}(g)
and
\widetilde{U}_{n,2}(g)
, respectively. As distinct observations from the sample
\mathbf{D}_{n}^{(l)}
always appear in distinct summands, by conditioning on all copies of the data other than the
l
th, the statistic (
2.12
) can be viewed as an average of independent random variables. Standard moment bounds for independent sums, in particular symmetrization and Khintchine-type inequalities, can therefore be applied over the randomness induced by the
l
th coordinate by conditioning in this way, while leaving the independence structure of the remaining coordinates intact. By iteratively applying a moment bound of this form, once in each coordinate, with each application contributing a factor of order
n^{-1/2}
, it is possible to obtain a moment bound for
\widetilde{U}_{n,m}(f^{(m)})
of order
n^{-m/2}
.
3
3

3

Each application of the symmetrization inequality requires
that the summands be mean-zero conditional on the remaining
coordinates. This holds at every iteration because the Hoeffding
component
f^{(m)}
is completely degenerate, in the sense that it has
mean zero conditional on any strict subset of its arguments. The
argument is therefore not applicable to
U
-statistics with general
kernels.
See, e.g.,
de la Pena, (1992)
,
Arcones and Giné, (1993)
, and
Sherman, (1994)
.

Distinct samples

(c)

Notes:
Figure

1
illustrates the construction of the statistics considered in the proof of
Lemma

2.2
, in the case
m=2
and
n=12
. Each panel displays a grid enumerating the pairs of indices
(i_{1},i_{2})
. Shaded cells indicate the pairs included in the corresponding sum. As each kernel under consideration is symmetric, ordered pairs are displayed. The bars adjacent to each axis indicate the sample indexed by the corresponding coordinate. Positions without a bar do not enter the sum. Panel A displays the
U
-statistic
U_{n,2}(g)
, which averages over all pairs of distinct indices of a single sample. Panel B displays the decoupled statistic
\widetilde{U}_{n,2}(g)
, defined in (
2.12
), which retains the same index set, but evaluates each coordinate of the kernel on an independent copy of the data. Panel C displays the decoupled statistic
\overline{U}_{n,2}(g)
, defined in (
2.14
), which averages over all pairs of the first
\lfloor n/2\rfloor
indices of two independent copies of the data. As the coordinates displayed in Panel C index distinct samples, pairs with
i_{1}=i_{2}
reference distinct observations. Panel D displays the statistic
U_{n,2}(g,\mathsf{R})
, defined in (
2.16
), which averages over the product of the disjoint random cells
\mathsf{r}_{1}
and
\mathsf{r}_{2}
of a single sample. The conditional expectation of the statistic displayed in Panel D, given the data
\mathbf{D}_{n}
, is equal to the statistic displayed in Panel A. The statistics displayed in Panels C and D are identically distributed.

The core challenge, then, is to show that the moments of the decoupled statistic (
2.12
) can be used to bound the moments of the Hoeffding component
U_{n,m}(f^{(m)})
. This is the role of a decoupling inequality. Inequalities of this form are the subject of a large literature, surveyed in Chapter 3 of
De la Pena and Giné, (1999)
. The sharpest available bound is due to
De la Peña and Montgomery-Smith, (1995)
, who show that

(2.13)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[|U_{n,m}(g)|^{q}\big]^{1/q}\leq h(m)\,\mathbb{E}\big[|\widetilde{U}_{n,m}(g)|^{q}\big]^{1/q}

holds for any symmetric kernel
g
, where the sequence
h(m)=m^{\Theta(m^{2})}
depends only on the order
m
. The deficiency of this bound is its dependence on
m
. If the order
m
is fixed, then
h(m)
can be absorbed into universal constants. In our setting, however, the decomposition (
2.5
) involves components of every order
2\leq m\leq b
. If
m
is non-negligible, the factor
m^{\Theta(m^{2})}
can grow much more quickly than the factor
n^{-m/2}
shrinks, and so bounds obtained from (
2.13
) are useful only if
b
grows very slowly with
n
.

We resolve this issue by developing the following new one-sided decoupling bound for higher-order moments of
U
-statistics. In contrast to the classical bound (
2.13
), the multiplicative factor appearing in this inequality is equal to one.

Lemma 2.3
.

Define the decoupled statistic

(2.14)

\displaystyle\overline{U}_{n,m}(g)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lfloor n/m\rfloor^{m}}\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}\cdots\sum_{i_{m}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}g(D_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\ldots,D_{i_{m}}^{(m)})

for each symmetric kernel
g:\mathcal{D}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
. The inequality

(2.15)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|U_{n,m}(g)|^{q}\right]^{1/q}\leq\mathbb{E}\left[|\overline{U}_{n,m}(g)|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

holds.

Proof.

Let
\mathsf{R}=(\mathsf{r}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{r}_{m},\tilde{\mathsf{r}})
be a random partition of
[n]
, such that each cell
\mathsf{r}_{i}
has cardinality
\lfloor n/m\rfloor
and the remainder cell
\tilde{\mathsf{r}}
has cardinality
n-m\lfloor n/m\rfloor
. Define the statistic

(2.16)

\displaystyle U_{n,m}(g,\mathsf{R})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lfloor n/m\rfloor^{m}}\sum_{i_{1}\in\mathsf{r}_{1}}\cdots\sum_{i_{m}\in\mathsf{r}_{m}}g(D_{i_{1}},\ldots,D_{i_{m}})\penalty\ .

Observe that the statistics (
2.14
) and
(
2.16
) are identically distributed; compare Panels C and D of
Figure

1
. In turn, observe that the elements of the fixed set
\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,m}
are assigned to distinct cells of the partition
\mathsf{r}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{r}_{m}
with probability

(2.17)

m!\frac{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}{n}\frac{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}{n-1}\cdots\frac{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}{n-m+1}=\lfloor n/m\rfloor^{m}{n\choose m}^{-1}\penalty\ .

Thus, we can evaluate

(2.18)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[U_{n,m}(g,\mathsf{R})\mid\mathbf{D}_{n}\right]

\displaystyle={n\choose m}^{-1}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,m}}g(D_{\mathsf{s}})=U_{n,m}(g)\penalty\ .

Consequently, as
q\geq 1
, Jensen’s inequality implies that

(2.19)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|U_{n,m}(g)|^{q}\right]^{1/q}\leq\mathbb{E}\left[|U_{n,m}(g,\mathsf{R})|^{q}\right]^{1/q}\penalty\ .

But then, as (
2.14
) and (
2.16
) are identically distributed, the bound (
2.15
) also holds.

Remark 2.2
.

The construction applied in the proof of
Lemma

2.3
is reminiscent of the representation

(2.20)

\displaystyle U_{n,m}(g)=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\bigg\lfloor\frac{n}{m}\bigg\rfloor^{-1}\sum_{l=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}g(D_{\mathsf{s}_{\pi,l}})\penalty\ ,

given in
Hoeffding, (1948)
, where
\mathcal{P}_{n}
denotes the set of permutations of
[n]
and
\mathsf{s}_{\pi,l}=\{\pi((l-1)m+1),\ldots,\pi(lm)\}
. In particular, Jensen’s inequality and the representation (
2.20
) imply that

(2.21)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\big[|U_{n,m}(g)|^{q}\big]^{1/q}\leq\mathbb{E}\Bigg[\bigg|\bigg\lfloor\frac{n}{m}\bigg\rfloor^{-1}\sum_{l=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}g(D_{\mathsf{s}_{\pi,l}})\bigg|^{q}\Bigg]^{1/q}\penalty\ ,

where, again, the multiplicative factor is equal to one. Bounds of this form are used to obtain the classical
Hoeffding, (1963)
inequality, whose leading term is displayed in (
1.7
).
\blacksquare

The essential feature of the bound (
2.15
) is that the dominating statistic (
2.14
) retains the structure that made the classical decoupled statistic (
2.12
) tractable; compare Panels B and C of

Figure

1
. Each argument of the kernel is again computed on its own independent copy of the data, and distinct observations from the
l
th copy again appear in distinct summands. The following Lemma illustrates that the iterative symmetrization argument described at the beginning of this section can thus be applied to
\overline{U}_{n,m}(g)
, without incurring a factor analogous to
h(m)
. We defer the proof to
Appendix

A
.

Lemma 2.4
.

For each
l
in
[m]
, let
V^{(l)}=(V^{(l)}_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
denote a collection of i.i.d. Rademacher variables.

(i)

If the symmetric function
g:\mathcal{D}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfies the condition

(2.22)

\mathbb{E}[g(D_{1},D_{2},\ldots,D_{m})\mid D_{2},\ldots,D_{m}]=0

almost surely, then the moment inequality

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|\overline{U}_{n,m}(g)|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

(2.23)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2^{m}}{\lfloor n/m\rfloor^{m}}\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}\cdots\sum_{i_{m}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}V_{i_{1}}^{(1)}\cdots V_{i_{m}}^{(m)}g(D_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\ldots,D_{i_{m}}^{(m)})\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

holds for each
q\geq 1
.

(ii)
Fix the deterministic real-valued array
\{g_{(i_{1},...,i_{m})}:i_{j}\in[\lfloor n/m\rfloor]\text{ for all }j\in[m]\}
. The moment inequality

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}\cdots\sum_{i_{m}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}V_{i_{1}}^{(1)}\cdots V_{i_{m}}^{(m)}g_{(i_{1},...,i_{m})}\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

(2.24)

\displaystyle\leq q^{m/2}\left(\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}\cdots\sum_{i_{m}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}g_{(i_{1},...,i_{m})}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

holds for each
q>2
.

Part (i) is obtained by an iterated application of a standard symmetrization inequality for sums of independent, centered random variables; see e.g., Section 6.4 of
Vershynin, (2018)
. Part (ii) is a direct specialization of the Bonami inequality for homogeneous Rademacher chaos, stated as Theorem 3.2.2 of
De la Pena and Giné, (1999)
; see also
Sherman, (1994)
.
4
4

4

A closely related symmetrization inequality, also based on
Sherman, (1994)
, is applied by
Song et al., (2019)
directly to the Hoeffding components
U_{n,m}(f^{(m)})
, without decoupling. Paired with the Bonami inequality, this argument again attains the scale
n^{-m/2}
for each component. However, a quadratic dependence on
b
in the resulting bounds arises due to their treatment of the analogue of the sum of squares appearing in (
2.25
). There, each summand is bounded by a quantity that does not depend on the order
m
, obtained from the tail condition placed on the kernel, so that the bound obtained for the sum is proportional to the number of summands. In fact, Hoeffding
orthogonality implies that the moments of the squared component
(f^{(m)})^{2}
decrease at the rate
\binom{b}{m}^{-1}
as the order
m
increases.
Lemma

2.5
exploits this decay, through an argument that relies on the decoupled structure of the statistic
\overline{U}_{n,m}(f^{(m)})
.
Minsker, (2023)
applies the same symmetrization inequality in a way analogous to
Song et al., (2019)
, with bounds on exponential moments in place of bounds on first moments, under substantially stronger smoothness conditions on the kernel.

To apply
Lemma

2.4
, recall that the Hoeffding projection
f^{(m)}
is completely degenerate, in the sense of the condition (
2.22
). Thus, Parts (i) and (ii) of
Lemma

2.4
and the law of iterated expectation imply that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|{b\choose m}\overline{U}_{n,m}(f^{(m)})\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2^{m}}{\lfloor n/m\rfloor^{m}}\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|{b\choose m}\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}\cdots\sum_{i_{m}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}V_{i_{1}}^{(1)}\cdots V_{i_{m}}^{(m)}f^{(m)}(D_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\ldots,D_{i_{m}}^{(m)})\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

(2.25)

\displaystyle\leq C^{m}q^{m/2}\lfloor n/m\rfloor^{-m}{b\choose m}\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}\cdots\sum_{i_{m}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}f^{(m)}(D_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\ldots,D_{i_{m}}^{(m)})^{2}\bigg|^{q/2}\right]^{1/q}\penalty\ .

Hence, by combining
Lemma

2.3
and (
2.25
), we obtain the inequality

(2.26)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|{b\choose m}U_{n,m}(f^{(m)})\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq C^{m}q^{m/2}\lfloor n/m\rfloor^{-m/2}{b\choose m}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\frac{1}{\lfloor n/m\rfloor^{m}}\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}\cdots\sum_{i_{m}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}f^{(m)}(D_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\ldots,D_{i_{m}}^{(m)})^{2}\bigg|^{q/2}\right]^{1/q}\penalty\ ,

for each
q>2
.

Thus, it suffices to give a suitable bound for the moment

(2.27)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\frac{1}{\lfloor n/m\rfloor^{m}}\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}\cdots\sum_{i_{m}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}f^{(m)}(D_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\ldots,D_{i_{m}}^{(m)})^{2}\bigg|^{q/2}\right]^{2/q}\penalty\ .

We do this through the application of the following Lemma, whose proof is again deferred to
Appendix

A
.

Lemma 2.5
.

(i)

Let
g:\mathcal{D}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
be a symmetric, non-negative kernel. For each integer
0\leq t\leq m
, define

(2.28)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{t,q}(g)=\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\mathbb{E}\left[g(D_{[m]})\mid D_{1},\ldots,D_{t}\right]\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}\penalty\ ,

where we adopt the convention that
\Gamma_{0,q}(g)=\mathbb{E}[g(D_{[m]})]
. The inequality

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\frac{1}{\lfloor n/m\rfloor^{m}}\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}\cdots\sum_{i_{m}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}g(D_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\ldots,D_{i_{m}}^{(m)})\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}\leq C^{m}\sum_{t=0}^{m}\left(\frac{q}{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}\right)^{t}\Gamma_{t,q}(g)

holds for each
q\geq 1\vee\log(\lfloor n/m\rfloor)
.

(ii)

Let
f:\mathcal{D}^{b}\to\mathbb{R}
be a symmetric kernel that satisfies the bound
\|f(D_{[b]})\|_{\psi_{1}}\leq\phi
. Then, for each integer
m\leq b
and
0\leq t\leq m
, the inequality

(2.29)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{t,q}((f^{(m)})^{2})\leq\phi^{2}(Cq)^{2}C^{t}{b\choose m}^{-1}\left(\frac{b}{m}\right)^{t}

holds for each
q\geq 1
.

Part (i) is a specialization of Proposition 2.1 of
Giné et al., (2000)
to large higher-order moments of symmetric non-negative kernels. The bound controls moments of the form (
2.27
) with a geometrically weighted series of conditional moments obtained by fixing subsets of arguments of the kernel under consideration. Part (ii) then gives a suitable bound for these conditional moments. The result follows by fixing the first
t
arguments of the kernel
f
and identifying the corresponding section of
f^{(m)}
as the
(m-t)
th Hoeffding component of the induced kernel. This representation then facilitates a further application of Hoeffding orthogonality. We are not aware of a direct antecedent for this estimate, although Hoeffding orthogonality has been used in a related way to control the variance of higher-order terms in Hoeffding expansions. See, for instance,
Athey et al., (2019)
and
Peng et al., (2022)
.

To complete the proof, observe that as
\log(n)\leq q/2
, Parts (i) and (ii) of
Lemma

2.5
imply

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\frac{1}{\lfloor n/m\rfloor^{m}}\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}\cdots\sum_{i_{m}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}f^{(m)}(D_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\ldots,D_{i_{m}}^{(m)})^{2}\bigg|^{q/2}\right]^{2/q}

(2.30)

\displaystyle\leq C^{m}\sum_{t=0}^{m}\left(\frac{q/2}{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}\right)^{t}\Gamma_{t,q/2}((f^{(m)})^{2})\leq C^{m}\phi^{2}q^{2}{b\choose m}^{-1}\sum_{t=0}^{m}\left(\frac{q/2}{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}\frac{Cb}{m}\right)^{t}\penalty\ .

Consequently, as
q\leq cn/b
and
b\leq n/2
, we obtain the bound

(2.31)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|{b\choose m}U_{n,m}(f^{(m)})\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq C^{m}q^{m/2+1}\phi\bigg\lfloor\frac{n}{m}\bigg\rfloor^{-m/2}{b\choose m}^{1/2}

by plugging (
2.30
) into (
2.26
) and applying the geometric series formula. Hence, as

(2.32)

\displaystyle{b\choose m}\leq\left(\frac{eb}{m}\right)^{m}\quad\text{and}\quad\bigg\lfloor\frac{n}{m}\bigg\rfloor^{-m/2}\leq\left(\frac{2m}{n}\right)^{m/2}\penalty\ ,

we obtain the bound

(2.33)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|{b\choose m}U_{n,m}(f^{(m)})\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq\phi q\left(\frac{Cqb}{n}\right)^{m/2}

for all
2\log(n)\leq q\leq cn/b
, as required.

3.
Simultaneous Inference for Subsampled Kernel Regression

In this section, we apply
Theorem

1.1
to establish the consistency of an approach for constructing resampling-based simultaneous confidence intervals around nonparametric regression estimators based on subsampled kernels. We begin in
Section

3.1
by defining and illustrating the confidence region under consideration. In
Sections

3.2
and
3.3
we define subsampled kernel regression and state several regularity conditions. The main result is given in
Section

3.4
. Throughout, for any function
f(x)
and vector
\bm{x}^{(d)}=(x^{(j)})_{j=1}^{d}
, we let
f(\bm{x}^{(d)})
denote the vector
(f(x^{(1)}),\ldots,f(x^{(d)}))
.

3.1.
Construction

The target for inference is the parameter vector
\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
, where
\theta_{0}(x)
is defined as the unique solution in
\theta
to the conditional moment equation

(3.1)

M(x;\theta,g_{0})=\mathbb{E}\left[m(D_{i};\theta,g_{0})\mid X_{i}=x\right]=0\penalty\ .

Here,
X_{i}
is a sub-vector of the observation
D_{i}
,
\bm{x}^{(d)}=(x^{(j)})_{j=1}^{d}
is a vector of query points, and
g_{0}
is a nuisance parameter. We focus on estimators
\hat{\theta}_{n}(x)
obtained as solutions to the empirical moment equation

(3.2)

M_{n}(x;\theta,\hat{g}_{n},\mathbf{D}_{n})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}K(x,X_{i})m(D_{i};\theta,\hat{g}_{n})=0\penalty\ ,

where
\hat{g}_{n}
is some first-stage estimator of
g_{0}
and
K(\cdot,\cdot)
is a data-dependent kernel constructed with subsampling.

Example 3.1
.

To fix concepts, consider
Banerjee et al., (2015)
, who study the effects of a poverty alleviation program implemented in Ghana.
5
5

5

Banerjee et al., (2015)
study data collected from several similar graduation programs. We focus on the data from their evaluation of the program implemented in Ghana.
Section

D.3
gives further details.
For each individual in their sample, they observe the data
D_{i}=(Y_{i},W_{i},Z_{i})
, where
Y_{i}
is a measurement of total assets taken two years after the implementation of the program,
W_{i}
is an indicator denoting assignment to the program, and
Z_{i}
is a vector of covariates. A broad aim of the study is to determine the conditions under which recipients of aid experience lasting improvements in welfare. One quantity that can inform this determination is the conditional average treatment effect (CATE)

(3.3)

\theta_{0}(x)=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[Y_{i}(1)-Y_{i}(0)\mid X_{i}=x\right]\penalty\ ,

where
Y_{i}(1)
and
Y_{i}(0)
are the potential outcomes generated by the intervention
W_{i}
and
X_{i}
is some chosen subvector of
Z_{i}
. Many modern approaches to estimating CATEs are premised on the observation that
\theta_{0}(x)
is the solution to the conditional moment equation

(3.4)

M(x;\theta,g_{0})=\mathbb{E}\left[(\mu_{1}(Z_{i})-\mu_{0}(Z_{i}))+\beta(W_{i},Z_{i})(Y_{i}-\mu_{W_{i}}(Z_{i}))-\theta\mid X_{i}=x\right]=0\penalty\ ,

where the nuisance parameter
g_{0}
collects the conditional outcome regression and Horvitz-Thompson weight

(3.5)

\mu_{w}(z)=\mathbb{E}_{P}\left[Y_{i}\mid W_{i}=w,Z_{i}=z\right]\quad\text{and}\quad\beta(w,z)=\frac{w}{\pi(z)}-\frac{1-w}{1-\pi(z)}\penalty\ ,

for the propensity score
\pi(z)=P\{W_{i}=1\mid Z_{i}=z\}
. See
Semenova and Chernozhukov, (2021)
,
Foster and Syrgkanis, (2023)
, and
Kennedy, (2023)
, for further discussion.

Figure

2
displays CATE estimates for the experiment studied in
Banerjee et al., (2015)
, where the chosen conditioning covariates
X_{i}
are pretreatment measurements of monthly consumption and total assets. The nuisance parameter estimate
\hat{g}_{n}
and the kernel
K(x,x^{\prime})
are constructed with the implementation of random forest regression made available through the “GRF” R package
(
Athey et al.,, 2019
)
. The graduation program appears to be most effective for individuals with a high level of baseline consumption and a low level of baseline assets.
6
6

6

The quartiles of baseline log consumption are 3.33, 3.76, and 4.20. The quartiles of baseline assets are -0.45, -0.71, and 0.03. Panel A of
Figure

F.3
displays a scatter plot of baseline log consumption and assets.
These results are suggestive of a poverty trap: individuals with an opportunity to increase their assets are able to do so only if they have a high level of baseline consumption
(
Balboni et al.,, 2022
;
Kraay and McKenzie,, 2014
)
.
\blacksquare

Figure 2.
CATE Estimates

Notes:
Figure

2
displays a heat map giving CATE estimates for the intervention studied in
Banerjee et al., (2015)
on post-treatment assets. The color of each rectangle indicates the estimate of the CATE queried at the rectangle’s central point. The horizontal and vertical axes display the baseline monthly consumption, normalized to 2014 US dollars and measured in logs base 10, and the baseline value of an index for total assets. CATE estimates are obtained by solving the empirical moment equation (
3.2
) for each value
x
on an evenly spaced grid on both axes. See
Section

D.3
for further details.

We consider a method for assessing the statistical significance of estimates typified by
Figure

2
. In particular, we study a simple procedure for constructing confidence intervals
\hat{\mathcal{C}}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
centered around the solutions to (
3.2
) that satisfy the bound

(3.6)

\sup_{P\in\mathbf{P}}\big|P\left\{\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\right\}-(1-\alpha)\big|\leq r_{n,d}

for a sequence
r_{n,d}
that converges to zero in regimes where
d
may increase more quickly than
n
and
\mathbf{P}
is some statistical family that contains the distribution
P
.
7
7

7

For the sake of simplicity, we consider only the case that the moment
m(\cdot;\theta,g_{0})
and the parameter
\theta
are scalar-valued. The vector-valued case is relevant, e.g., when estimating CATEs with multiple treatment variables. Our results generalize to the vector-valued case at the cost of additional notation.

To this end, let
\mathsf{h}
denote a random element of
\mathcal{S}_{n,n/2}
, i.e., a random half-sample of
[n]
and let
\hat{\theta}_{\mathsf{h}}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
denote the vector of the solutions to (
3.2
), with the data
\mathbf{D}_{n}
replaced by the half-sample
D_{\mathsf{h}}
. Our construction is premised on approximating the distribution of the root

(3.7)

R_{n}(\bm{x}^{(d)})=\hat{\theta}_{n}(\bm{x}^{(d)})-\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})

with the conditional distribution of the half-sample bootstrap root

(3.8)

R^{*}_{n}(\bm{x}^{(d)})=\hat{\theta}_{\mathsf{h}}(\bm{x}^{(d)})-\hat{\theta}_{n}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\penalty\ .

The nuisance parameter estimator
\hat{g}_{n}
does not need to be re-estimated when computing (
3.8
). A version of the half-sample bootstrap is implemented by default in the GRF R package
(
Athey et al.,, 2019
)
. The half-sample bootstrap is an instance of subsampling
(
Politis and Romano,, 1994
;
Politis et al.,, 1999
)
.

Let
\hat{\lambda}^{2}_{n,j}
denote the variance of
\sqrt{n}R^{*}_{n}(x^{(j)})
, conditioned on the data
\mathbf{D}_{n}
, and let
\hat{\mathsf{cv}}_{n}(\alpha)
denote the
1-\alpha
quantile of the distribution of the studentized statistic

(3.9)

\hat{S}^{*}_{n}(\bm{x}^{(d)})=\sqrt{n}\left\|\hat{\Lambda}_{n}^{-1/2}R^{*}_{n}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\right\|_{\infty}\penalty\ ,

again conditioned on the data
\mathbf{D}_{n}
. Here,
\hat{\Lambda}_{n}
denotes the diagonal matrix with elements
\hat{\lambda}^{2}_{n,j}
.
8
8

8

The quantities
\hat{\lambda}^{2}_{n,j}
and
\widehat{\mathsf{cv}}_{n}(\alpha)
are easily approximated by resampling the bootstrap root (
3.8
). To simplify exposition, we omit explicit consideration of residual randomness induced by this approximation.
We consider confidence intervals with the following structure.

Definition 3.1
.

Define the intervals

(3.10)

\hat{\mathcal{C}}(x^{(j)})=\hat{\theta}_{n}(x^{(j)})\pm n^{-1/2}\hat{\lambda}_{n,j}\widehat{\mathsf{cv}}_{n}(\alpha)\quad\text{for each}\quad j\text{ in }[d]\penalty\ .

The level-
\alpha
half-sample confidence region for
\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
is given by
\hat{\mathcal{C}}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
.

Confidence intervals with the same structure, based on different choices of bootstrap root, are studied in, e.g.,
Chernozhukov et al., (2014)
and
Belloni et al., (2018)
.

The essential feature of the bootstrap root (
3.8
) is that it can be computed without knowing anything about the structure of the estimator
\hat{\theta}_{n}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
. In particular, approaches based on the Rademacher or Gaussian multiplier bootstrap rely on knowledge of a linear approximation to
\hat{\theta}_{n}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
. To gain intuition, suppose that the estimator
\hat{\theta}_{n}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
satisfies the exact linear representation

(3.11)

\hat{\theta}_{n}(\bm{x}^{(d)})=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overline{u}(\bm{x}^{(d)},D_{i})

for some function
\overline{u}(\cdot,\cdot)
. Let
V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}
denote a collection of random variables, where
V_{i}
takes the value
1
if the index
i
is an element of the random set
\mathsf{h}
used to define the half-sample bootstrap root (
3.8
), and takes the value
-1
otherwise. Observe that

\displaystyle R^{*}_{n}(\bm{x}^{(d)})=\hat{\theta}_{\mathsf{h}}(\bm{x}^{(d)})-\hat{\theta}_{n}(\bm{x}^{(d)})

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{I}\{i\in\mathsf{h}\}\overline{u}(\bm{x}^{(d)},D_{i})-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overline{u}(\bm{x}^{(d)},D_{i})

(3.12)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}\left(\overline{u}(\bm{x}^{(d)},D_{i})-\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\right)\penalty\ .

The weights
V_{i}
are exchangeable Rademacher random variables, i.e., they are uniformly distributed on
\{1,-1\}
. If the weights were fully independent, the representation (
3.12
) reduces to the more familiar Rademacher bootstrap. The representation (
3.12
) is due to
Yadlowsky et al., (2023)
, who draw on a similar observation made in the context of two-sample testing in
Chung and Romano, (2013)
.

In practice, many widely applied estimators are not perfectly linearly decomposable. Often, however, estimators do satisfy an approximate linear decomposition, in the sense that the equality (
3.11
) holds with a remainder term of order, say,
o_{p}(n^{-\gamma})
for some positive constant
\gamma
. As we will see, estimators constructed with subsampled kernels are approximately linear around some unknown function
\overline{u}(\cdot,\cdot)
. If an estimator
\hat{\theta}_{n}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
is approximately linear, then the subsampled estimate
\hat{\theta}_{\mathsf{h}}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
is
immediately
also approximately linear.
9
9

9

Approximate linearity does not immediately imply a representation analogous to (
3.12
) for a root constructed with an empirical bootstrap, as approximate linearity would not necessarily hold under the empirical distribution.
Thus, for approximately linear statistics, the representation (
3.12
) continues to hold, now with a remainder term of order
o_{p}(n^{-\gamma})
.

Example 3.2

(continues=ex: banerjee)
.

We now return to the data studied in
Banerjee et al., (2015)
.
Figure

3
displays upper and lower half-sample confidence bounds for the CATE (
3.3
) on post-treatment assets. The null hypothesis that the CATE is equal to zero is only rejected for individuals with low baseline assets. The lower bounds are meaningfully larger than zero only for individuals with low baseline assets and high baseline consumption. That is, the graduation program has a positive impact on individuals who do not have many assets to begin with, but who do have access to a stable source of consumption. On the other hand, the confidence regions contain zero for individuals who have low baseline consumption or high baseline assets. In
Section

3.5
, we measure the coverage and average length of the confidence region
\hat{\mathcal{C}}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
in a simulation calibrated to this setting.
\blacksquare

Figure 3.
Half-Sample Confidence Region

Panel A: Upper Bound

Panel B: Lower Bound

Notes:
Figure

3
displays heat maps giving half-sample upper and lower confidence bounds for the CATE of the intervention studied in
Banerjee et al., (2015)
on post-treatment total assets. The confidence bounds are constructed at level
\alpha=0.1
. The upper and lower bounds are displayed with different color palettes to emphasize the use of different scales. A contour line has been superimposed over the lower bound to demarcate where the bound crosses zero. The axes and estimator are the same as in
Figure

2
.

3.2.
Subsampled Kernel Regression

Subsampled kernel regression is a broad class of algorithms for solving regression problems of the form (
3.2
), based on constructing a data-driven kernel function
K(x,x^{\prime})
with subsampling. Formally, fix some positive integer
r
and let
(\mathsf{s}_{q})_{q=1}^{r}
collect a sequence of subsets of
[n]
drawn independently and uniformly from
\mathcal{S}_{n,b}
. Let
\xi
denote some auxiliary source of randomness and let
(\xi_{\mathsf{s}_{q}})_{q=1}^{r}
collect a set of independent random variables with the same distribution as
\xi
. We study conditional empirical moment estimators of the form (
3.2
), where the kernel function
K(x,x^{\prime})
admits the decomposition

(3.13)

K(x,X_{i})=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{q=1}^{r}\mathbb{I}\{i\in\mathsf{s}_{q}\}\kappa(x,X_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}_{q}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}_{q}})

for some known kernel
\kappa(\cdot,\cdot,D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})
. Several widely applied instances of subsampled kernels are as follows.
10
10

10

The half-sample bootstrap is particularly computationally efficient for estimators constructed with subsampling, as the estimator and the half-sampled estimator can be constructed using the same collection of subsamples. See Section 4.1 of
Athey et al., (2019)
.

Example 3.3
.

Subsampled nearest-neighbors regression is a simple example of a kernel with the structure (
3.13
)
(
Fix and Hodges,, 1989
)
. Here, the kernel
\kappa(x,X_{i^{\prime}},D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})
is non-zero if and only if
X_{i^{\prime}}
is one of the
k
closest points to
x
among the points in the subsample
D_{\mathsf{s}}
.

Example 3.4
.

Random forest regression, introduced by
Breiman, (2001)
, is another example of a kernel with the structure (
3.13
). In this case, each pair
(D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})
generates some partition of
\mathcal{X}
. The kernel
\kappa(x,x^{\prime},D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})
is non-zero if and only if
x
and
x^{\prime}
are in the same element of the partition generated by
(D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})
. Often, such partitions are constructed with recursive algorithms, e.g., the “CART” algorithm of
Breiman et al., (1984)
.

We impose the following restrictions on the kernels under consideration.

Assumption 3.1

(Honesty and Positive Symmetry)
.

(i) The kernel
\kappa(\cdot,\cdot,D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi)
is Honest in the sense that

(3.14)

\kappa(x,X_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})\perp\!\!\!\perp m(D_{i};\theta,g)\mid X_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}_{-i}}\penalty\ ,

where
\perp\!\!\!\perp
denotes conditional independence and
\mathsf{s}_{-i}
denotes the set
\mathsf{s}\setminus\{i\}
.

(ii) The kernel
\kappa(\cdot,\cdot,D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi)
is positive and satisfies the restriction
\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}\kappa(\cdot,X_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})=1
almost surely. Moreover, the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}\left[\kappa(\cdot,X_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})\mid D_{\mathsf{s}}\right]
is invariant to permutations of the data
D_{\mathsf{s}}
.

The “Honesty” condition stipulated in Part (i) of
Assumption

3.1
imposes the restriction that any part of the data
D_{i}
that can affect the value of the moment
m(D_{i};\theta,g)
cannot affect the value of the kernel
\kappa(x,X_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})
. This condition was introduced in
Athey and Imbens, (2016)
. Honesty is often achieved through kernel construction schemes based on sample-splitting; see
Wager and Athey, (2018)
and
Athey et al., (2019)
for further discussion. Part (ii) of
Assumption

3.1
imposes several weak regularity conditions.

The following two quantities restrict the “size” and “variability” of the chosen kernel.

Definition 3.2

(Shrinkage and Incrementality)
.

Let
\mathsf{s}
be an arbitrary element of
\mathcal{S}_{n,b}
and let
l
be any element of
\mathsf{s}
.

(i) We say that the kernel
\kappa(\cdot,\cdot,D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})
has a uniform shrinkage rate
\varepsilon_{b}
if

(3.15)

\sup_{P\in\mathbf{P}}\sup_{j\in[d]}\mathbb{E}\left[\max\left\{\|X_{i}-x^{(j)}\|_{\infty}:\kappa(x^{(j)},X_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})>0\right\}\right]\leq\varepsilon_{b}\penalty\ .

(ii) We say that the kernel
\kappa(\cdot,\cdot,D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})
is uniformly incremental if

(3.16)

\inf_{P\in\mathbf{P}}\inf_{j\in[d]}\Var\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}\kappa(x^{(j)},X_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})m(D_{i};\theta,g)\mid D_{l}=D\right]\right)\gtrsim b^{-1}

where
D
is an independent random variable with distribution
P
.

The shrinkage rate of a kernel
\kappa(\cdot,\cdot,D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})
is analogous to the bandwidth of a classic deterministic kernel. The incrementality restriction ensures that the chosen kernel is not overly dependent on a single data point. Both notions were introduced by
Wager and Athey, (2018)
and have been characterized explicitly for various widely applied subsampled kernel estimators.
11
11

11

The terminology “shrinkage” was introduced in
Oprescu et al., (2019)
, and is not intended to connote (explicit) regularization.

Example 3.5

(continues=ex: knn)
.

In the case of honest subsampled
k
-NN regression,
Khosravi et al., (2019)
show that
\varepsilon_{b}\lesssim b^{-1/p}
, where
p
is the intrinsic dimension of the measure of the covariates
X_{i}
. See e.g.,
Kpotufe, (2011)
for further discussion. In turn,
Khosravi et al., (2019)
and
Peng et al., (2022)
show that the kernels associated with both honest and non-honest variants of
k
-NN regression are incremental, up to logarithmic factors that depend on the dimension of the covariates.
\blacksquare

Example 3.6

(continues=ex: random forest)
.

Analogously, for honest random forest regression, under suitable regularity conditions,
Wager and Athey, (2018)
establish that
\varepsilon_{b}\lesssim b^{-c/p}
, where
p
is the dimension of the domain of
X_{i}
. See e.g.,
Wager and Athey, (2018)
and
Oprescu et al., (2019)
for further discussion. Bounds adaptive to the intrinsic dimension of the measure of
X_{i}
are given in
Huo et al., (2023)
.
Wager and Athey, (2018)
and
Peng et al., (2022)
give simple conditions under which the kernel associated with subsampled, honest, random forest regression is uniformly incremental, again up to dimension-dependent logarithmic factors.
\blacksquare

3.3.
Moment Restrictions

We restrict attention to conditional moments that satisfy a Neyman orthogonality condition
(
Chernozhukov et al.,, 2018
)
. We assume that the nuisance parameter
g_{0}
is a collection of
h
real-valued functions
g_{0}=(g_{0}^{(k)})_{k=1}^{h}
, each having domain
\mathcal{Z}
given by the support of the covariates
Z_{i}
. Define the norm

(3.17)

\|g-g_{0}\|_{2,\infty}=\sup_{k\in[h]}\sup_{j\in[d]}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[(g^{(k)}(Z_{i})-g_{0}^{(k)}(Z_{i}))^{2}\mid X_{i}=x^{(j)}\right]\right)^{1/2}

for any
g=(g^{(k)})_{k=1}^{h}
. The nuisance parameter
g_{0}
takes values in the space
\mathcal{G}
. Throughout, for a functional
F
on
\mathcal{F}
, we use the notation

\partial_{f}F(f)[h]=\frac{\text{d}}{\text{d}t}F(f+th)\big|_{t=0}\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{f,f}F(f)[h]=\frac{\text{d}^{2}}{\text{d}t^{2}}F(f+th)\big|_{t=0}

to denote first- and second- order directional derivatives, respectively.

Definition 3.3

(Local Neyman Orthogonality)
.

We say that a conditional moment
M(\cdot;\theta_{0},g_{0})
is uniformly locally Neyman orthogonal if

(3.18)

\partial_{g}M(x^{(j)};\theta_{0},g_{0})[g-g_{0}]=0

for all
P
in
\mathbf{P}
and
x^{(j)}
in
\bm{x}^{(d)}
.

The use of Neyman orthogonal moments ensures that the bias induced by the estimation of the nuisance parameter
g_{0}
with
\hat{g}_{n}
is small
(
Chernozhukov et al.,, 2018
)
.

In addition to Neyman orthogonality, we require several smoothness restrictions on the function
m(\cdot;\theta,g)
. In the main text, to ease exposition, we impose the following linearity and boundedness restriction.

Assumption 3.2

(Moment Linearity and Boundedness)
.

The moment function
m(\cdot;\theta,g)
satisfies the linear representation

(3.19)

m(D_{i};\theta,g)=m^{(1)}(D_{i};g)\cdot\theta+m^{(2)}(D_{i};g)

for some known functions
m^{(1)}(\cdot;g)
and
m^{(2)}(\cdot;g)
. Moreover, the absolute value of the function
m(\cdot;\theta,g)
is bounded by the constant
(|\theta|+1)\phi
for some
\phi\geq 1
almost surely.

The linearity restriction entailed in
Assumption

3.2
is inessential and is imposed for the sake of simplicity.
12
12

12

In
Appendix

B
, we show that moment linearity can be replaced by the high-level assumption that
\hat{\theta}_{n}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
is consistent for
\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
. This state of affairs is standard in
M
-estimation problems
(
Newey and McFadden,, 1994
, see e.g., )
. As our running examples use linear moments, and sufficient conditions for the consistency of
\hat{\theta}_{n}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
have been established
(
Oprescu et al.,, 2019
, see e.g., Assumption 4.1 and Theorem 4.3 of )
), we omit a detailed consideration of nonlinear moments.
Like in
Corollary

1.2
, the boundedness restriction can be weakened to a slightly more involved assumption stated in terms of sub-exponential norms. Again, we impose boundedness to ease exposition.

We maintain the following additional mild smoothness restrictions.

Assumption 3.3

(Moment Smoothness)
.

(i)
The moment
M(\cdot;\theta,g_{0})
is uniformly second order smooth, in the sense that

(3.20)

\sup_{P\in\mathbf{P}}\sup_{j\in[d]}\big|\partial_{g,g}M(x^{(j)};\theta_{0},g_{0})[g-g_{0}]\big|\lesssim\|g-g_{0}\|^{2}_{2,\infty}

for each
g
in
\mathcal{G}
.

(ii)
The variogram

V(x;g)=\mathbb{E}\left[(m(D_{i};\theta_{0}(x),g)-m(D_{i};\theta_{0}(x),g_{0}))^{2}\mid X=x\right]

is uniformly Lipschitz in its first component, in the sense that

(3.21)

\sup_{P\in\mathbf{P}}\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\big|V(x;g)-V(x^{\prime};g)\big|\lesssim\|x-x^{\prime}\|_{\infty}

holds for all
x
and
x^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{X}
. Moreover, the variogram satisfies the mean-squared continuity condition

(3.22)

\sup_{P\in\mathbf{P}}\sup_{j\in[d]}|V(x^{(j)},g)-V(x^{(j)},g^{\prime})|\lesssim\|g-g^{\prime}\|^{2}_{2,\infty}

for each
g
and
g^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{G}
.

(iii)
Define the moments

(3.23)

\displaystyle M^{(1)}(x;\theta,g)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[m^{(1)}(D_{i};g)\mid X_{i}=x\right]\quad\text{and}

(3.24)

\displaystyle M^{(2)}(x;g)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[m^{(2)}(D_{i};g)\mid X_{i}=x\right]\penalty\ ,

associated with the functions
m^{(1)}(\cdot;g)
and
m^{(2)}(\cdot;g)
introduced in
Assumption

3.2
. Both moments are
uniformly Lipschitz in their first component. That is, it holds that

(3.25)

\sup_{P\in\mathbf{P}}\sup_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\big|M^{(1)}(x;g)-M^{(1)}(x^{\prime};g)\big|\lesssim\|x-x^{\prime}\|_{\infty}

for all
x
,
x^{\prime}
, and
\theta
, and analogously for
M^{(2)}(x;g)
. Moreover, the first moment is uniformly Lipschitz in its second component and bounded from below in the sense that

(3.26)

\displaystyle\sup_{P\in\mathbf{P}}\sup_{j\in[d]}\big|M^{(1)}(x^{(j)};\theta_{0},g)-M^{(1)}(x^{(j)};g_{0})\big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\|g-g_{0}\|_{2,\infty}\quad\text{and}

(3.27)

\displaystyle\inf_{P\in\mathbf{P}}\inf_{j\in[d]}\big|M^{(1)}(x^{(j)};g)\big|

\displaystyle\geq c

for each
g
and
\theta
and some positive constant
c
.

(iv)
The parameter space
\Theta
is a bounded, convex subset of
\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, the target parameter satisfies
\theta_{0}(x)\in\Theta
for every
x\in\mathcal{X}
and every
P\in\mathbf{P}
and the estimator
\hat{\theta}_{n}(x)
is constructed as a solution over
\Theta
.

Neyman orthogonal moments satisfying the smoothness restrictions specified in
Assumption

3.2
and
Assumption

3.3
are available for many widely considered statistical problems. For example, the moment (
3.4
) is the unique Neyman orthogonal identifying moment for the parameter (
3.3
)
(
Hahn,, 1998
;
Chernozhukov et al.,, 2018
, see e.g.,)
.
13
13

13

For estimation of CATEs, Part (i) of
Assumption

3.3
is implied by the more refined bound

(3.28)

\sup_{P\in\mathbf{P}}\sup_{j\in[d]}\big|\partial_{g,g}M(x^{(j)};\theta_{0},g_{0})[g-g_{0}]\big|\lesssim\|\mu-\mu_{0}\|_{2,\infty}\|\beta-\beta_{0}\|_{2,\infty}\penalty\ ,

where
\mu
and
\beta
are defined in (
3.5
). This structure yields the celebrated “Double Robustness” result for estimation of conditional average treatment effects; see
Chernozhukov et al., (2018)
and
Kennedy, (2023)
for further discussion. We impose the more general condition (
3.20
), as this bound exhibits many of the same features and will hold for a wider variety of problems.
Additional examples of problems where smooth Neyman orthogonal identifying moments are available include estimation of partially linear regression and partially linear instrumental variable regression
(
Chernozhukov et al.,, 2018
)
, local average treatment effects
(
Tan,, 2006
;
Frölich,, 2007
)
, dynamic treatment effects
(
Lewis and Syrgkanis,, 2021
)
, and long-term treatment effects identified by surrogate outcomes
(
Athey et al.,, 2020
;
Chen and Ritzwoller,, 2023
)
.

3.4.
Coverage Accuracy

The following theorem gives a bound on the error in the nominal coverage probability of the confidence regions introduced in
Definition

3.1
. We emphasize that the result is applicable to asymptotic regimes where the dimension of the query-vector
\bm{x}^{(d)}
can be exponentially larger than the sample size
n
.

Theorem 3.1
.

Suppose that the kernel
\kappa(\cdot,\cdot,D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})
satisfies
Assumption

3.1
, has uniform shrinkage rate
\varepsilon_{b}
, and is uniformly incremental. Additionally, suppose that the Neyman orthogonal moment function
M(\cdot;\theta_{0},g_{0})
satisfies
Assumption

3.2
and
Assumption

3.3
and that
r
has been chosen to satisfy
n\leq b\sqrt{r}
. If the estimator
\hat{g}_{n}
is statistically independent of the data
\mathbf{D}_{n}
and satisfies the moment bound

(3.29)

\displaystyle\sup_{P\in\mathbf{P}}\mathbb{E}\left[\|\hat{g}_{n}-g_{0}\|_{2,\infty}^{q}\right]^{1/q}\leq q\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}

for some sequence
\delta_{n,g}
and each
q\geq 2\vee 2\log(dn)
, then the confidence region given in
Definition

3.1
satisfies

\displaystyle\sup_{P\in{\mathbf{P}}}\big|P\left\{\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\right\}-(1-\alpha)\big|

(3.30)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\lesssim\left(\frac{b\phi^{4}\log^{8}(dn)}{n}\right)^{1/4}+\left(\delta_{n,g}^{2}+\sqrt{\frac{n}{b}}\varepsilon_{b}\right)\log^{3}(dn)\penalty\ .

Remark 3.1
.

To expedite exposition, in stating
Theorem

3.1
, we have assumed that the nuisance parameter estimator
\hat{g}_{n}
is computed on a separate sample, independent of the data
\mathbf{D}_{n}
. This can be achieved by randomly splitting the available data into two subsamples. The first can be used to construct the nuisance parameter estimate
\hat{g}_{n}
; the second to construct the confidence region
\hat{\mathcal{C}}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
. There are practical issues with this strategy. First, the region
\hat{\mathcal{C}}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
might be sensitive to the choice of the split of the data
(
Ritzwoller and Romano,, 2023
)
. Second, by splitting the data, researchers incur a potentially meaningful loss in statistical precision. In
Section

D.1
we give additional conditions under which the nuisance parameter estimator
\hat{g}_{n}
can be computed using the same data used to construct the region
\hat{\mathcal{C}}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
.
14
14

14

Our analysis builds on
Chen et al., (2022)
, who give a pointwise analysis of unconditional moment estimators.
If these conditions are not plausible for a given application, in
Section

D.2
, we detail a modified version of the confidence region given in
Definition

3.1
that is constructed with cross-splitting.
\blacksquare

Remark 3.2
.

The bound (
3.30
) can be interpreted as a bias-variance decomposition. The first term in (
3.30
) results from a Berry-Esseen-type bound, analogous to
Corollary

1.2
, on the accuracy of a normal approximation to (
3.31
). The term involving the kernel shrinkage
\varepsilon_{b}
is a remnant of a bound on the supremum of the bias of the estimator
\hat{\theta}_{n}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
.
\blacksquare

Remark 3.3
.

Theorem

3.1
follows from an application of an abstract result stated in
Appendix

B
. The proof is given in
Appendix

C
. The abstract result applies to conditional moment estimators that are not necessarily constructed with subsampled kernels. Rather, the result holds under the high-level assumption that the estimator
\hat{\theta}_{n}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
is approximately linear, with a sufficiently small remainder term, and has sufficiently small bias and stochastic equicontinuity.

\blacksquare

The proof of
Theorem

3.1
has three main steps. First, through a standard series of expansions
(
Chernozhukov et al.,, 2018
;
Oprescu et al.,, 2019
, see e.g.,)
, we show that the root
R_{n}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
can be approximated by

(3.31)

\displaystyle W_{n,b}(\bm{x}^{(d)})

\displaystyle={n\choose b}^{-1}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}\mathbb{E}\left[u(\bm{x}^{(d)};D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}},\theta_{0},g_{0})\mid D_{\mathsf{s}}\right]\penalty\ ,\quad\text{where}

\displaystyle u(x;D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}},\theta,g)

\displaystyle=-M^{(1)}(x;g_{0})^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}\big(\kappa(x,X_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})m(D_{i};\theta,g)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad-\mathbb{E}\big[\kappa(x,X_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})m(D_{i};\theta,g)\big]\big)\penalty\ ,

with a remainder term given by the second term in (
3.30
). The quantity (
3.31
) can be recognized as a
U
-statistic of order
b
. Second, we apply
Theorem

1.1
to show that this quantity is approximately linear. Written differently, we establish that the root
R_{n}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
satisfies a linear representation of the form (
3.11
), up to a small remainder term. It immediately follows that the bootstrap root
R^{*}_{n}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
satisfies the linear representation (
3.12
), up to a small remainder term. Finally, we conclude by applying suitable central limit theorems
(
Chernozhukov et al.,, 2022
)
to the linear terms (
3.11
) and (
3.12
).

The broadened scope of
Theorem

1.1
is an essential input into making
Theorem

3.1
operational. To see this, recall that for many honest subsampled kernel estimators, the shrinkage rate
\varepsilon_{b}
satisfies the bound
\varepsilon_{b}\lesssim b^{-c/p}
for some small constant
c
and some integer
p
measuring the dimension of the covariates
X_{i}
. Suppose that

(3.32)

b=n^{\gamma_{b}}\quad\text{and}\quad\|\hat{g}_{n}-g_{0}\|_{2,\infty}\lesssim n^{-\gamma_{g}}\penalty\ ,

with probability greater than
1-1/n
, for some constants
\gamma_{b}
and
\gamma_{g}
between
0
and
1
. In this case, ignoring logarithmic factors and other constants, the bound (
3.30
) can be re-expressed as

(3.33)

n^{\frac{1}{4}(\gamma_{b}-1)}+n^{\frac{1}{2}(1-\gamma_{b}-4\gamma_{g})}+n^{\frac{1}{2}(1-\gamma_{b}(1+c/p))}\penalty\ .

In other words, the confidence region defined in
Definition

3.1
is consistent if

(3.34)

1\leq\gamma_{b}+4\gamma_{g}\quad\text{and}\quad 1\leq\gamma_{b}(1+c/p)\penalty\ ,

and so the bound (
3.30
) only converges if

(3.35)

\displaystyle\gamma_{b}\geq\frac{p}{p+c}\penalty\ .

Written differently, it is essential to accommodate subsample exponents
\gamma_{b}
very close to one, i.e., the regime
b=o(n)
. This is exactly the range enabled by
Theorem

1.1
.

3.5.
Simulation

We now measure the performance of the confidence region formulated in
Definition

3.1
. We apply a method for simulation design proposed by
Athey et al., (2021)
. In particular, we calibrate a simulation to the
Banerjee et al., (2015)
data using a Generative Adversarial Network (GAN)
(
Goodfellow et al.,, 2014
)
. In effect, we construct a data generating process that approximates the
Banerjee et al., (2015)
data, where we know the true value of the CATE
\theta_{0}(x)
queried at each value
x
used to construct the grids displayed in
Figures

2
and
3
. Further details are given in
Section

D.4
.

Measurements of performance are taken as two parameters vary. First, we consider several values of the sample size
n
. In particular, we consider settings with
n=h\cdot n_{0}
, for
h
in
\{1,2.5,5,7.5\}
, where
n_{0}
is the sample size of the
Banerjee et al., (2015)
data. Second, we vary the proportion
b/n
. We consider three regimes:
b/n=0.05
,
b/n=(2/(h+1))0.05
, and
b/n=(1/h)0.05
. Observe that
b
increases in proportion to
n
in the first regime and that
b
is constant as
n
varies in the third regime. The second regime resides between these two extremes.

Figure 4.
Performance

Notes:

4
displays several measurements of the performance of the confidence intervals formulated in
Definition

3.1
in a simulation calibrated to the
Banerjee et al., (2015)
data. The nominal level is
\alpha=0.1
; a horizontal dotted line is displayed at the nominal coverage
1-\alpha
in the first panel. The confidence bounds considered are constructed analogously to the confidence bounds displayed in
Figure

3
. The
x
-axis of each panel is the sample multiplier
h
. The color of each measurement varies with
b/n
.

4
displays measurements of the coverage and width of the confidence region formulated in
Definition

3.1
, in addition to measurements of the bias of the estimator
\hat{\theta}_{n}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
. Here, we consider the setting where the nuisance parameter estimator
\hat{g}_{n}
is computed using the same data used to construct the region
\hat{\mathcal{C}}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
. The first row displays measurements of the coverage of the confidence region, the coverage of the lower bound (i.e., Panel B of
Figure

3
), and the coverage of the upper bound (i.e., Panel A of
Figure

3
). The nominal level is
\alpha=0.1
. Throughout, the confidence region is somewhat conservative.

The second row of
Figure

4
illustrates a bias-variance trade-off with the subsample size
b
. The first panel displays measurements of the average width of the confidence region. Here, the average is taken over both simulation draws and the query-vector
\mathbf{x}^{(d)}
. The width of the confidence region is increasing in the proportion
b/n
and is essentially constant if
b/n
is constant as
n
increases. By contrast, the second and third panels display the maximum and average bias of the estimator
\hat{\theta}_{n}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
, again taken over the query-vector
\mathbf{x}^{(d)}
. The bias is decreasing in the proportion
b/n
and is essentially constant if
b
is constant as
n
varies.

Contents

Appendix A
Proofs for Results Supporting
Theorem

1.1

A.1.
Proof of
Lemma

2.4

A.1.1.
Part (i)

The result follows from an iterative application of the following symmetrization inequality.

Lemma A.1

(
Vershynin, (2018)
, Exercise 6.4.5)
.

Let
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}
be independent, mean-zero, real-valued random variables. If
V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}
denote an independent sequence of i.i.d. Rademacher random variables, then the inequality

(A.1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}\leq 2\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{n}V_{i}Z_{i}\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

holds for each
q\geq 1
.

In particular, observe that we can write

(A.2)

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}\cdots\sum_{i_{m}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}g(D_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\ldots,D_{i_{m}}^{(m)})=\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}Z^{(1)}_{i_{1}}

where the summands

(A.3)

\displaystyle Z^{(1)}_{i}=\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}\cdots\sum_{i_{m}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}g(D_{i}^{(1)},D_{i_{2}}^{(2)},\ldots,D_{i_{m}}^{(m)})\penalty\ .

are independently distributed conditional on the replications
\mathbf{D}_{n}^{(2)},\ldots,\mathbf{D}_{n}^{(m)}
. Moreover, the complete degeneracy condition (
2.22
) implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Z^{(1)}_{i}\mid\mathbf{D}_{n}^{(2)},\ldots,\mathbf{D}_{n}^{(m)}]

(A.4)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}\cdots\sum_{i_{m}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}\mathbb{E}[g(D_{i}^{(1)},D_{i_{2}}^{(2)},\ldots,D_{i_{m}}^{(m)})\mid D_{i_{2}}^{(2)},\ldots,D_{i_{m}}^{(m)}]=0\penalty\ .

Thus, by applying
Lemma

A.1
conditional on
\mathbf{D}_{n}^{(2)},\ldots,\mathbf{D}_{n}^{(m)}
and taking expectations, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\frac{1}{\lfloor n/m\rfloor^{m}}\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}\cdots\sum_{i_{m}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}g(D_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\ldots,D_{i_{m}}^{(m)})\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

(A.5)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\lfloor n/m\rfloor^{m}}\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}\cdots\sum_{i_{m}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}V_{i_{1}}^{(1)}g(D_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\ldots,D_{i_{m}}^{(m)})\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}\penalty\ .

Hence, by iterating the same argument
m-1
more times, first conditionally on all blocks except
\mathbf{D}_{n}^{(2)}
, then on all blocks except
\mathbf{D}_{n}^{(3)}
, and so on, we can conclude that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\frac{1}{\lfloor n/m\rfloor^{m}}\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}\cdots\sum_{i_{m}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}g(D_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\ldots,D_{i_{m}}^{(m)})\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

(A.6)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2^{m}}{\lfloor n/m\rfloor^{m}}\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}\cdots\sum_{i_{m}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}V_{i_{1}}^{(1)}\cdots V_{i_{m}}^{(m)}g(D_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\ldots,D_{i_{m}}^{(m)})\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}\penalty\ ,

as required.

A.1.2.
Part (ii)

The result can be obtained as a specialization of the following standard moment inequality for homogeneous Rademacher chaos, often referred to as the Bonami inequality.

Lemma A.2

(Theorem 3.2.2,
De la Pena and Giné, (1999)
)
.

Fix a collection of real-valued quantities
\{z_{\mathsf{s}}:\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}\}
and let
V_{1},\ldots,V_{n}
denote an independent collection of Rademacher random variables. Consider the homogeneous Rademacher chaos of order
b
, given by

(A.7)

Z_{b}=\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}V_{\mathsf{s}}z_{\mathsf{s}}\penalty\ ,

where
V_{\mathsf{s}}=\Pi_{i\in\mathsf{s}}V_{i}
for each subset
\mathsf{s}
in
[n]
. The moment inequality

(A.8)

\mathbb{E}\left[|Z_{b}|^{q}\right]\leq q^{bq/2}(\Delta_{b})^{q/2}\penalty\ ,\quad\text{where}\quad\Delta_{b}=\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}(z_{\mathsf{s}})^{2}\penalty\ ,

holds for every
q>2
.

To apply
Lemma

A.2
, we require some further notation. In particular, let
\overline{n}=\lfloor n/m\rfloor\cdot m
, define the injection

(A.9)

\displaystyle h:[m]\times[\lfloor n/m\rfloor]\to[\overline{n}]\quad\text{by}\quad h(l,i)=(l-1)\lfloor n/m\rfloor+i\penalty\ ,

and enumerate the sets

(A.10)

\displaystyle\mathsf{s}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{m})=\{h(1,i_{1}),\ldots,h(m,i_{m})\}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{\overline{n},m}

for each
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{m})
in
[\lfloor n/m\rfloor]^{m}
. Observe that the mapping
(i_{1},\ldots,i_{m})\mapsto\mathsf{s}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{m})
is a bijection. Thus, we can define the array

(A.11)

\displaystyle z_{\mathsf{s}}=\begin{cases}g_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{m}}\penalty\ ,&\text{if }\mathsf{s}=\mathsf{s}(i_{1},\ldots,i_{m})\text{ for some }(i_{1},\ldots,i_{m})\in[\lfloor n/m\rfloor]^{m}\penalty\ ,\\
0\penalty\ ,&\text{otherwise}\penalty\ ,\end{cases}

and the sequence of i.i.d. Rademacher variables

(A.12)

\displaystyle V_{h(l,i)}=V_{i}^{(l)}\penalty\ ,

for each
l\in[m]
and
i\in[\lfloor n/m\rfloor]
, such that

(A.13)

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}\cdots\sum_{i_{m}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}V_{i_{1}}^{(1)}\cdots V_{i_{m}}^{(m)}g_{(i_{1},...,i_{m})}=\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{\overline{n},m}}V_{\mathsf{s}}z_{\mathsf{s}}

almost surely. Therefore,
Lemma

A.2
implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}\cdots\sum_{i_{m}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}V_{i_{1}}^{(1)}\cdots V_{i_{m}}^{(m)}g_{(i_{1},...,i_{m})}\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{\overline{n},m}}V_{\mathsf{s}}z_{\mathsf{s}}\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

(A.14)

\displaystyle\leq q^{m/2}\left(\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{\overline{n},m}}z_{\mathsf{s}}^{2}\right)^{1/2}\leq q^{m/2}\left(\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}\cdots\sum_{i_{m}=1}^{\lfloor n/m\rfloor}g_{(i_{1},...,i_{m})}^{2}\right)^{1/2}

for each
q>2
, as required.

A.2.
Proof of
Lemma

2.5

A.2.1.
Part (i)

It will suffice to establish the following more general statement. Fix integers
m\geq 1
and
k\geq 1
and let
g:\mathcal{D}^{m}\to\mathbb{R}_{+}
be a symmetric, non-negative kernel. Define the quantity

(A.15)

\displaystyle A_{m,k}(g)=\frac{1}{k^{m}}\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{k}\cdots\sum_{i_{m}=1}^{k}g(D_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\ldots,D_{i_{m}}^{(m)})\penalty\ .

The inequality

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|A_{m,k}(g)|^{q}\right]^{1/q}\leq C^{m}\sum_{t=0}^{m}\left(\frac{q}{k}\right)^{t}\Gamma_{t,q}(g)

holds for each
q\geq 1\vee\log(k)
.

We proceed by induction on
m
. To verify the base case, associated with
m=1
, it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|A_{1,k}(g)|^{q}\right]^{1/q}=\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{k}g(D_{i_{1}}^{(1)})\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\lesssim\Gamma_{0,q}(g)+\frac{q}{k}\Gamma_{1,q}(g)

(A.16)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[g(D_{i_{1}}^{(1)})]+\frac{q}{k}\mathbb{E}\left[|g(D_{i_{1}}^{(1)})|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

holds for each
q\geq\log(k)
. This bound follows immediately from the following specialization of Rosenthal’s inequality for non-negative random variables.

Lemma A.3
.

Let
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{k}
be i.i.d. non-negative random variables. Then, the inequality

(A.17)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}Z_{i}\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}\lesssim\mathbb{E}[Z_{1}]+\frac{q}{k}\mathbb{E}\left[|Z_{1}|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

holds for each
q\geq 1\vee\log(k)
.

Proof.

Rosenthal’s inequality for non-negative random variables, stated as Theorem 15.10 in
Boucheron et al., (2013)
, implies that

(A.18)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{k}Z_{i}\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}\lesssim k\mathbb{E}[Z_{1}]+q\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{i\in[k]}|Z_{i}|^{q}\right]^{1/q}\penalty\ .

As
q\geq\log(k)
, we have that
k^{1/q}\leq e
, and therefore

(A.19)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\max_{i\in[k]}|Z_{i}|^{q}\right]^{1/q}\leq k^{1/q}\mathbb{E}\left[|Z_{i}|^{q}\right]^{1/q}\leq e\mathbb{E}\left[|Z_{i}|^{q}\right]^{1/q}\penalty\ .

The result follows by plugging (
A.19
) into (
A.18
) and dividing by
k
.

Thus, it suffices to verify the inductive step. That is, assume that the bound (
A.2.1
) has been established for kernels of order
m-1
. Observe that we can write

(A.20)

\displaystyle A_{m,k}(g)=\frac{1}{k^{m}}\sum_{i_{1}=1}^{k}\cdots\sum_{i_{m}=1}^{k}g(D_{i_{1}}^{(1)},\ldots,D_{i_{m}}^{(m)})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{{i_{1}}=1}^{k}Z^{(1)}_{i_{1}}

where

(A.21)

\displaystyle Z^{(1)}_{i_{1}}=\frac{1}{k^{m-1}}\sum_{i_{2}=1}^{k}\cdots\sum_{i_{m}=1}^{k}g(D^{(1)}_{i_{1}},D^{(2)}_{i_{2}},\ldots,D^{(m)}_{i_{m}})\penalty\ .

Conditional on the replicates
\mathbf{D}_{n}^{(2)},\ldots,\mathbf{D}_{n}^{(m)}
, the variables
Z^{(1)}_{1},\ldots,Z^{(1)}_{k}
are i.i.d. and non-negative. Therefore,
Lemma

A.3
implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|A_{m,k}(g)|^{q}\mid\mathbf{D}_{n}^{(2)},\ldots,\mathbf{D}_{n}^{(m)}\right]^{1/q}

(A.22)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}[Z^{(1)}_{1}\mid\mathbf{D}_{n}^{(2)},\ldots,\mathbf{D}_{n}^{(m)}]+\frac{q}{k}\mathbb{E}\left[|Z^{(1)}_{1}|^{q}\mid\mathbf{D}_{n}^{(2)},\ldots,\mathbf{D}_{n}^{(m)}\right]^{1/q}\penalty\ .

Hence, Minkowski’s inequality implies that

(A.23)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|A_{m,k}(g)|^{q}\right]^{1/q}\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}[Z^{(1)}_{1}\mid\mathbf{D}_{n}^{(2)},\ldots,\mathbf{D}_{n}^{(m)}]^{q}\right]^{1/q}+\frac{q}{k}\mathbb{E}\left[|Z^{(1)}_{1}|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

as
q\geq 1
. Now, by defining the
m-1
order kernels

(A.24)

\displaystyle g_{0}(d_{2},\ldots,d_{m})=\mathbb{E}[g(D_{1},d_{2},\ldots,d_{m})]\quad\text{and}\quad g_{d}(d_{2},\ldots,d_{m})=g(d,d_{2},\ldots,d_{m})

we can identify

(A.25)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Z^{(1)}_{1}\mid\mathbf{D}_{n}^{(2)},\ldots,\mathbf{D}_{n}^{(m)}]=A_{m-1,k}(g_{0})\quad\text{and}\quad Z^{(1)}_{1}=A_{m-1,k}(g_{D^{(1)}_{i_{1}}})\penalty\ ,
