. We begin by verifying the requisite assumptions.
Theorem

B.1
requires that the moment function
M(\cdot;\theta_{0},g_{0})
is Neyman Orthogonal and satisfies
Assumption

B.1
. Neyman Orthogonality is also imposed in the conditions of
Theorem

3.1
, and so is satisfied.
Assumption

B.1
requires that the moment function
M(x;\theta_{0},g_{0})
is twice continuously differentiable and has a Jacobian function

(C.1)

M^{(1)}(x;g)=\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta^{\prime}}M(x;\theta,g)\mid_{\theta^{\prime}=\theta}

that satisfies the bounds (
B.2
) and (
B.3
). Twice continuous differentiability
follows immediately from
Assumption

3.2
, which states that the moment function
m(\cdot;\theta,g)
satisfies the linear representation

(C.2)

m(d;\theta,g)=m^{(1)}(d;g)\cdot\theta+m^{(2)}(d;g)\penalty\ ,

with bounded functions
m^{(1)}(\cdot;g)
and
m^{(2)}(\cdot;g)
. In particular, the representation (
C.2
) implies that the Jacobian can be written

(C.3)

M^{(1)}(x;\theta,g)=\mathbb{E}[m^{(1)}(D_{i};g)\mid X_{i}=x]

and that the Hessian can be written

(C.4)

H(x;\theta,g)=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial^{2}\theta^{\prime}}M(x;\theta,g_{0})\mid_{\theta^{\prime}=\theta}=0\penalty\ .

Here, our ability to exchange integration and differentiation follows from boundedness of
m(\cdot;\theta,g)
. Consequently, the bounds (
B.2
) and (
B.3
) follow from the bounds (
3.26
) and (
3.27
) stated as Part (iii) of
Assumption

3.3
.

Theorem

B.1
also requires that the centered empirical moment
\overline{M}_{n}(x;\theta,g)
satisfy
Assumption

B.2
.
Assumption

B.2
imposes the restriction that
\overline{M}_{n}(x;\theta,g)
is twice continuously differentiable and that its Hessian

(C.5)

\displaystyle\overline{H}_{n}(x;\theta,g)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial^{2}\theta^{\prime}}\overline{M}_{n}(x;\theta^{\prime},g_{0})|_{\theta^{\prime}=\theta}

is uniformly bounded almost surely as
x
,
\theta
, and
g
vary over their respective domains. Recall that, in our case, we have that

(C.6)

\overline{M}_{n}(x;\theta,g)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(K(x,X_{i})m(D_{i};\theta,g)-\mathbb{E}\left[K(x,X_{i})m(D_{i};\theta,g)\right]\right)\penalty\ .

Twice continuous differentiability again follows from
Assumption

3.2
, i.e., the linear representation (
C.2
). In turn, we have that
\overline{H}_{n}(x;\theta,g)=0
almost surely, verifying the uniform boundedness condition. Moreover, for future reference, the empirical Jacobian is given by

(C.7)

\overline{M}_{n}^{(1)}(x;g)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(K(x,X_{i})m^{(1)}(D_{i};g)-\mathbb{E}\left[K(x,X_{i})m^{(1)}(D_{i};g)\right]\right)

again by
Assumption

3.2
.

We now quantify the generic sequence
\delta_{n}^{u}
defined in
Assumption

B.3
. Recall the normalized statistic
W_{n}(x)
defined in (
B.6
). By the definition (
3.13
), this quantity can be written

\displaystyle W_{n}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{q=1}^{r}u(x;D_{\mathsf{s}_{q}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}_{q}},\theta_{0},g_{0})\penalty\ ,\quad\text{where}

\displaystyle u(x;D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}},\theta,g)

\displaystyle=-M^{(1)}(x;g_{0})^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}\big(\kappa(x,X_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})m(D_{i};\theta,g)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad-\mathbb{E}\big[\kappa(x,X_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})m(D_{i};\theta,g)\big]\big)\penalty\ .

Define the de-randomized kernel function and Hájek projection

(C.8)

\displaystyle\tilde{u}(x;D)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[u(x;D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}},\theta_{0}(x),g_{0})\mid D_{\mathsf{s}}=D\right]\quad\text{and}

(C.9)

\displaystyle\tilde{u}^{(1)}(x;D)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[u(x;D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}},\theta_{0}(x),g_{0})\mid i\in\mathsf{s},D_{i}=D\right]\penalty\ ,

respectively, in addition to the quantities

(C.10)

\sigma^{2}_{b,j}=\Var(\tilde{u}^{(1)}(x^{(j)},D_{i}))\quad\text{and}\quad\underline{\sigma}^{2}_{b}=\min_{j\in[d]}\sigma^{2}_{b,j}\penalty\ .

The result below follows from an application of
Theorem

1.1
.

Lemma C.1

(Asymptotic Linearity)
.

Suppose that the de-randomized kernel function (
C.8
) is bounded by
\phi\geq 1
almost surely and is invariant to permutations of its second argument. Suppose that
b
and
r
are chosen to satisfy
n\leq\sqrt{r}b
. If the inequality

(C.11)

\frac{(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\phi b\log(dn)}{n}\leq C

holds, then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\sqrt{\frac{n}{b^{2}\underline{\sigma}^{2}_{b}}}\left(W_{n}(x^{(j)})-\frac{b}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{u}^{(1)}(x^{(j)};D_{i})\right)\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

(C.12)

\displaystyle\lesssim q^{2}\left(\frac{(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\varphi^{2}\|_{\infty})^{2}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}_{b}n}\right)^{1/2}\penalty\ ,

for each
j\in[d]
and each
2\vee 2\log(nd)\leq q\leq cn/b
.

In particular, observe that
Assumption

3.2
implies that the de-randomized kernel function (
C.8
) is bounded by
\phi
, up to a constant that depends only on
\mathbf{P}
. Permutation invariance again follows by assumption. We may assume that the first condition in (
C.11
) holds, as otherwise the desired bound is vacuously true. Consequently,
Assumption

B.3
holds with the choice

(C.13)

\delta_{n,u}=C\left(\frac{b\phi^{2}}{n}\right)^{1/2}

by incrementality and the fact that
\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty}
is bounded as
P
varies over
\mathbf{P}
.

Next, we quantify the sequences introduced in
Assumption

B.4
. The lemma below follows from arguments similar to those used in
Wager and Athey, (2018)
and
Oprescu et al., (2019)
.

Lemma C.2

(Bias, Consistency, and Stochastic Equicontinuity)
.

Suppose that the conditions of
Theorem

3.1
are satisfied.

(i)
The bound

(C.14)

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{n}{b^{2}\underline{\sigma}_{b}^{2}}}\|\mathsf{Bias}_{n}(\bm{x}^{(d)};\theta(\bm{x}^{(d)}),\hat{g}_{n})\|_{\infty}\lesssim(1+\|\theta(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\delta_{n,B}\penalty\ ,\quad\text{where}

\displaystyle\delta_{n,B}=\left(\frac{n}{b^{2}\underline{\sigma}_{b}^{2}}\right)^{1/2}\varepsilon_{b}

holds.

(ii)
The bound

(C.15)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{n}{b^{2}\underline{\sigma}_{b}^{2}}\right)^{1/4}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\overline{M}^{(1)}_{n}(x^{(j)};g_{0})\right|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\lesssim q\delta_{n,m}\penalty\ ,\quad\text{where}\quad\delta_{n,m}=\left(\frac{\phi^{4}}{\underline{\sigma}_{b}^{2}n}\right)^{1/4}

holds for each
q\geq 2\vee 2\log(dn)
satisfying

(C.16)

\frac{\phi^{2}bq^{2}}{n}\leq c

and each
j\in[d]
.

(iii)
The bounds

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{n}{b^{2}\underline{\sigma}_{b}^{2}}}\mathbb{E}\left[\big|\overline{M}_{n}(x^{(j)};\theta_{0}(x^{(j)}),\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{M}_{n}(x^{(j)};\theta_{0}(x^{(j)}),g_{0})\big|^{q}\right]^{1/q}\lesssim q\delta_{n,S}\penalty\ ,\quad\text{where}

(C.17)

\displaystyle\delta_{n,S}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{n}{b^{2}\underline{\sigma}_{b}^{2}}\right)^{1/2}\bigg(\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}+\varepsilon_{b}+(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\phi\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\bigg)\penalty\ ,

and

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{n}{b^{2}\underline{\sigma}_{b}^{2}}}\mathbb{E}\left[\big|\overline{M}^{(1)}_{n}(x^{(j)};\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{M}^{(1)}_{n}(x^{(j)};g_{0})\big|^{q}\right]^{1/q}\lesssim q\delta_{n,J}\penalty\ ,\quad\text{where}

(C.18)

\displaystyle\delta_{n,J}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{n}{b^{2}\underline{\sigma}_{b}^{2}}\right)^{1/2}\bigg(\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}+\varepsilon_{b}+(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\phi\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\bigg)

hold for each
q\geq 2\vee 2\log(dn)
satisfying

(C.19)

\frac{\phi^{2}(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})^{2}bq^{2}}{n}+\varepsilon_{b}\leq c

and each
j\in[d]
.

(iv)
The bound

\displaystyle\left(\frac{n}{b^{2}\underline{\sigma}_{b}^{2}}\right)^{1/4}\mathbb{E}\left[\big|\hat{\theta}_{n}(x^{(j)})-\theta_{0}(x^{(j)})\big|^{q}\right]^{1/q}\lesssim q\delta_{n,\theta}\penalty\ ,\quad\text{where}

(C.20)

\displaystyle\delta_{n,\theta}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{n}{b^{2}\underline{\sigma}_{b}^{2}}\right)^{1/4}\bigg(\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}+(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\varepsilon_{b}+(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\phi\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\bigg)

holds for each
q\geq 2\vee 2\log(dn)
satisfying

(C.21)

\varepsilon_{b}\leq 1\penalty\ ,\quad q\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}\leq 1\penalty\ ,\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{\phi^{2}(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})^{2}bq^{2}}{n}+\varepsilon_{b}\leq 1

and each
j\in[d]
.

Observe that the choice

\overline{u}(x,D_{i})=b\cdot\tilde{u}^{(1)}(x,D_{i})\penalty\ ,

suggested by
Lemma

C.1
implies that

(C.22)

b\phi^{2}\gtrsim\underline{\lambda}^{2}=b^{2}\underline{\sigma}^{2}_{b}\gtrsim b\penalty\ ,

by
Assumption

3.2
and incrementality. We set
\overline{q}_{n,d}=C\log(dn)
. We may assume that there exists some
c\leq 1
such that

(C.23)

\left(\delta_{n,g}^{2}+\sqrt{\frac{n}{b}}\varepsilon_{b}\right)\log^{3}(dn)\leq c\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{b\phi^{4}\log^{8}(dn)}{n}\leq c\penalty\ ,

as otherwise the bound is vacuously true. Consequently, all of the conditions of
Lemma

C.2
are satisfied for every
q\leq\overline{q}_{n,d}
. Thus,
Lemma

C.2
and
Lemma

C.1
imply that we can set

(C.24)

\displaystyle\delta_{n,u}

\displaystyle=C\left(\frac{\phi^{2}}{\underline{\sigma}_{b}^{2}n}\right)^{1/2}\lesssim\left(\frac{b\phi^{2}}{n}\right)^{1/2}\penalty\ ,

(C.25)

\displaystyle\delta_{n,B}

\displaystyle=C\sqrt{\frac{n}{b^{2}\underline{\sigma}^{2}_{b}}}\varepsilon_{b}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{n}{b}}\varepsilon_{b}\penalty\ ,

(C.26)

\displaystyle\delta_{n,m}

\displaystyle=C\left(\frac{\phi^{4}}{\underline{\sigma}_{b}^{2}n}\right)^{1/4}\lesssim\left(\frac{b\phi^{4}}{n}\right)^{1/4}\penalty\ ,

\displaystyle\delta_{n,\theta}

\displaystyle=C\left(\frac{n}{b^{2}\underline{\sigma}^{2}_{b}}\right)^{1/4}\left(\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}+\varepsilon_{b}+\phi\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\right)

(C.27)

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta_{n,g}+\left(\frac{n}{b}\right)^{1/4}\varepsilon_{b}+\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)^{1/4}\phi\penalty\ ,\quad\text{and}

(C.28)

\displaystyle\delta_{n,S}=\delta_{n,J}

\displaystyle=C\log^{1/2}(dn)\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}+C\sqrt{\frac{n}{b}}\varepsilon_{b}+C\phi\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\log(dn)\penalty\ ,

respectively, where we have repeatedly used the fact that
\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty}
is uniformly bounded.

With these results in place, we apply
Theorem

B.1
. We begin by giving a suitable upper bound for the sequence

(C.29)

\delta_{n}=\delta^{2}_{n,g}+\delta^{2}_{n,\theta}+\delta^{2}_{n,m}+\delta_{n,B}+\delta_{n,S}+\delta_{n,u}+\underline{\lambda}^{1/2}n^{-1/4}\delta_{n,\theta}\left(\delta_{n,B}+\delta_{n,J}\right)

introduced in the statement of
Theorem

B.1
. Observe that

(C.30)

\displaystyle\frac{\underline{\lambda}^{1/2}}{n^{1/4}}(\delta_{n,B}+\delta_{n,J})\delta_{n,\theta}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{b^{1/4}}{n^{1/4}}(\delta_{n,B}+\delta_{n,J})\delta_{n,\theta}\lesssim\delta_{n,B}+\delta_{n,J}\penalty\ ,

as otherwise the desired bound would be vacuous, where the first inequality follows from (
C.22
). Consequently, we find that, if
\delta_{n,J}=\delta_{n,S}
, then

(C.31)

\delta_{n}\lesssim\delta^{2}_{n,g}+\delta^{2}_{n,\theta}+\delta^{2}_{n,m}+\delta_{n,B}+\delta_{n,S}+\delta_{n,u}\penalty\ .

Plugging in the choices specified above gives

(C.32)

\displaystyle\delta_{n}

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta_{n,g}^{2}+\sqrt{\frac{n}{b}}\varepsilon_{b}+\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\phi^{2}+\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}\log^{1/2}(dn)+\phi\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\log(dn)

(C.33)

\displaystyle\lesssim\delta_{n,g}^{2}+\sqrt{\frac{n}{b}}\varepsilon_{b}+\phi^{2}\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\log(dn)\penalty\ .

where, to get the second inequality, we apply the elementary bound

\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}\log^{1/2}(dn)\lesssim\delta_{n,g}^{2}+\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\log(dn)

and use
\phi\geq 1
. Hence, the inequality (
C.33
), the Orlicz-norm bound

\|\overline{u}(x^{(j)},D)\|_{\psi_{1}}\leq b\cdot(1+|\theta_{0}(x^{(j)})|)\phi\lesssim b\phi\penalty\ ,

and
Theorem

B.1
imply that

\displaystyle\sup_{P\in\mathbf{P}}\big|P\left\{\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\in\hat{\mathcal{C}}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\right\}-(1-\alpha)\big|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{b\phi^{2}\log^{5}(dn)}{n}\right)^{1/4}+\left(\delta_{n,g}^{2}+\sqrt{\frac{n}{b}}\varepsilon_{b}+\phi^{2}\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\log(dn)\right)\log^{3}(dn)+\frac{1}{nd}

(C.34)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{b\phi^{4}\log^{8}(dn)}{n}\right)^{1/4}+\left(\delta_{n,g}^{2}+\sqrt{\frac{n}{b}}\varepsilon_{b}\right)\log^{3}(dn)\penalty\ ,

as required.

C.2.
Proof of
Lemma

C.1

To ease notation, we define the parameter

\phi(\theta_{0})=(1+\|\theta_{0}(x^{(j)})\|)\phi

and drop dependence on
\theta(x)
and
g
when writing
u(x;D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}},\theta(x),g)
, as these values will be taken to be
\theta_{0}(x)
and
g_{0}
throughout. We are interested in studying the discrepancy

(C.35)

W_{n}(x^{(j)})-\frac{b}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{u}^{(1)}(x^{(j)};D_{i})\penalty\ .

Define the quantities

(C.36)

\displaystyle\hat{U}_{n}(x^{(j)})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{b}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}u(x^{(j)};D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{U}_{n}(x^{(j)})=\frac{1}{N_{b}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}\tilde{u}(x^{(j)};D_{\mathsf{s}})

for some collection of independent random variables
\bm{\xi}=(\xi_{\mathsf{s}})_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}

having the same distribution as
\xi
. The statistics
\hat{U}_{n}(x^{(j)})

and
\overline{U}_{n}(x^{(j)})
are the complete, randomized and de-randomized,

U
-statistics associated with the randomized
d
-dimensional kernel
u(x^{(j)};\cdot,\cdot)
,
respectively. Consider the decomposition

\displaystyle\sqrt{\frac{n}{b^{2}\underline{\sigma}^{2}_{b}}}(W_{n}(x^{(j)})-\frac{b}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{u}^{(1)}(x^{(j)};D_{i}))

\displaystyle\quad\quad=\sqrt{\frac{n}{b^{2}\underline{\sigma}^{2}_{b}}}(W_{n}(x^{(j)})-\hat{U}_{n}(x^{(j)}))+\sqrt{\frac{n}{b^{2}\underline{\sigma}^{2}_{b}}}(\hat{U}_{n}(x^{(j)})-\overline{U}_{n}(x^{(j)}))

(C.37)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+\sqrt{\frac{n}{b^{2}\underline{\sigma}^{2}_{b}}}(\overline{U}_{n}(x^{(j)})-\frac{b}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{u}^{(1)}(x^{(j)};D_{i}))\penalty\ .

We bound each of the three terms in (
C.37
) in succession.

A higher-order moment bound for the third term in (
C.37
) is obtained by applying the same argument used to verify
Lemma

C.1
. In particular, Part (ii) of
Lemma

2.1
and
Lemma

C.1
imply that

(C.38)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\overline{U}_{n}(x^{(j)})-\frac{b}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{u}^{(1)}(x^{(j)};D_{i})\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\lesssim\phi q^{2}\frac{b}{n}

for each
2\vee 2\log(nd)\leq q\leq cn/b
. Analogous bounds for the first two terms in (
C.37
) are obtained through the application of the following Lemma.

Lemma C.3
.

Let
\mathbf{D}_{n}=(D_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
and
\bm{\xi}=(\xi_{\mathsf{s}})_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}
denote two independent collections of independent and identically distributed random variables. Consider the real-valued function
u(D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})
. Assume that the absolute value of each component of
u(D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})
is bounded by the constant
\phi\geq 1
almost surely.

(i)
If the bound

(C.39)

q^{1/2}\frac{\phi b}{n}\leq 1

holds for some
q\geq 2\vee 2\log(dn)
, then the bound

(C.40)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\ {n\choose b}^{-1}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}\left(u(D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})-\mathbb{E}\left[u(D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})\mid\mathbf{D}_{\mathsf{s}}\right]\right)\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}\lesssim q^{1/2}\frac{\phi b}{n}

holds for all
b>2
.

(ii)
If, in addition, the sets
(\mathsf{s}_{q})_{q=1}^{r}
are drawn independently and identically from
\mathcal{S}_{n,b}
and
n\leq\sqrt{r}b
, then

(C.41)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\frac{1}{r}\sum_{q=1}^{r}\left(u(D_{\mathsf{s}_{q}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}_{q}})-\mathbb{E}\left[u(D_{\mathsf{s}_{q}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}_{q}})\mid\mathbf{D}_{n},\bm{\xi}\right]\right)\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}\lesssim q^{1/2}\frac{b\phi}{n}

holds.

In particular, observe that

\hat{U}_{n}(x^{(j)})=\frac{1}{N_{b}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}u(x^{(j)};D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})=\mathbb{E}[u(x^{(j)};D_{\mathsf{s}_{g}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}_{g}})\mid\mathbf{D}_{n},\bm{\xi}]

and that therefore we can write

W_{n}(x^{(j)})-\hat{U}_{n}(x^{(j)})=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{q=1}^{r}Z_{q},\quad\text{with}\quad Z_{q}=u(x^{(j)};D_{\mathsf{s}_{q}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}_{q}})-\mathbb{E}[u(x^{(j)};D_{\mathsf{s}_{q}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}_{q}})\mid\mathbf{D}_{n},\bm{\xi}]\penalty\ .

Consequently, by the normalization
q^{1/2}\phi(\theta_{0})b/n\leq 1
, Part (ii) of
Lemma

C.3
implies that

(C.42)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\sqrt{\frac{n}{b^{2}\underline{\sigma}^{2}_{b}}}\left(W_{n}(x^{(j)})-\hat{U}_{n}(x^{(j)})\right)\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{n}{b^{2}\underline{\sigma}^{2}_{b}}}q^{1/2}\frac{b\phi(\theta_{0})}{n}=\left(\frac{\phi(\theta_{0})^{2}q}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}_{b}n}\right)^{1/2}\penalty\ .

In turn, we can similarly write

\hat{U}_{n}(x^{(j)})-\overline{U}_{n}(x^{(j)})=\frac{1}{N_{b}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}Z_{\mathsf{s}}\quad\text{with}\quad Z_{\mathsf{s}}=u(x^{(j)};D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})-\mathbb{E}\left[u(x^{(j)};D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})\mid D_{\mathsf{s}}\right]\penalty\ .

Thus, again by the normalization
q^{1/2}\phi(\theta_{0})b/n\leq 1
, Part (i) of
Lemma

C.3
implies that

(C.43)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\sqrt{\frac{n}{b^{2}\underline{\sigma}^{2}_{b}}}\left(\hat{U}_{n}(x^{(j)})-\overline{U}_{n}(x^{(j)})\right)\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}\lesssim\left(\frac{\phi(\theta_{0})^{2}q}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}_{b}n}\right)^{1/2}\penalty\ .

Putting the pieces together, the bounds (
C.38
), (
C.42
), and (
C.43
) imply that

(C.44)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\sqrt{\frac{n}{b^{2}\underline{\sigma}^{2}_{b}}}\left(W_{n}(x^{(j)})-\frac{b}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\tilde{u}^{(1)}(x^{(j)};D_{i})\right)\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}\lesssim q^{2}\left(\frac{\phi(\theta_{0})^{2}}{\underline{\sigma}^{2}_{b}n}\right)^{1/2}\penalty\ ,

for each
j\in[d]
and each
2\vee 2\log(nd)\leq q\leq cn/b
, as required.

C.3.
Proof of
Lemma

C.2

Throughout, we use the shorthand
N_{b}={n\choose b}
.

C.3.1.
Part (i)

Observe that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[M_{n}(x;\theta,g)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}K(x,X_{i})m(D_{i};\theta,g)\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{b}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}\mathbb{E}\left[\kappa(x,X_{i},\mathsf{s},\xi)m(D_{i};\theta,g)\right]

(Honesty)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{b}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\kappa(x,X_{i},\mathsf{s},\xi)\mid X_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}_{-i}}\right]\mathbb{E}\left[m(D_{i};\theta,g)\mid X_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}_{-i}}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{b}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}\mathbb{E}\left[\kappa(x,X_{i},\mathsf{s},\xi)\mathbb{E}\left[m(D_{i};\theta,g)\mid X_{i}\right]\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{b}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}\mathbb{E}\left[\kappa(x,X_{i},\mathsf{s},\xi)M(X_{i};\theta,g)\right]\penalty\ .

Therefore, the normalization

(C.45)

\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}\kappa(x,X_{i},\mathsf{s},\xi)=1

implies that

\mathsf{Bias}_{n}(x;\theta,g)=\frac{1}{N_{b}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}\mathbb{E}\left[\kappa(x,X_{i},\mathsf{s},\xi)\left(M(X_{i};\theta,g)-M(x;\theta,g)\right)\right]\penalty\ .

By the boundedness part of
Assumption

3.2
and Part (iii) of
Assumption

3.3
, we find that

(C.46)

\mathsf{Bias}_{n}(x;\theta,g)\lesssim(1+|\theta|)\mathbb{E}\left[\kappa(x,X_{i},\mathsf{s},\xi)\|X_{i}-x\|_{\infty}\right]\leq(1+|\theta|)\varepsilon_{b}\penalty\ ,

where the final inequality follows from the definition of the shrinkage
\varepsilon_{b}
and the normalization (
C.45
).

C.3.2.
Part (ii)

Define the function

J(x;D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})=\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}\left(\kappa(x,X_{i},\mathsf{s},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})m^{(1)}(D_{i};g_{0})-\mathbb{E}\left[\kappa(x,X_{i},\mathsf{s},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})m^{(1)}(D_{i};g_{0})\right]\right)

and observe that

(C.47)

\overline{M}_{n}^{(1)}(x;g_{0})=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{q=1}^{r}J(x;D_{\mathsf{s}_{q}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}_{q}})\penalty\ .

Consider the decomposition

(C.48)

\overline{M}_{n}^{(1)}(x;g_{0})=\tilde{A}(x)+\hat{A}(x)+\overline{A}(x)\penalty\ ,

where

(C.49)

\displaystyle\tilde{A}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{q=1}^{r}\left(J(x;D_{\mathsf{s}_{q}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}_{q}})-\mathbb{E}\left[J(x;D_{\mathsf{s}_{q}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}_{q}})\mid\mathbf{D}_{n},\bm{\xi}\right]\right)\penalty\ ,

(C.50)

\displaystyle\hat{A}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{b}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}\left(J(x;D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})-\mathbb{E}\left[J(x;D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})\mid D_{\mathsf{s}}\right]\right)\penalty\ ,\quad\text{and}

(C.51)

\displaystyle\overline{A}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{b}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}\mathbb{E}\left[J(x;D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})\mid D_{\mathsf{s}}\right]\penalty\ ,

respectively. We again apply
Lemma

C.3
to bound (
C.49
) and (
C.50
). In particular, by
Assumption

3.2
, the normalization (
C.16
), and the restriction
n\leq b\sqrt{r}
,
Lemma

C.3
implies that, for each
j\in[d]
,

(C.52)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\tilde{A}(x^{(j)})\right|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\lesssim q^{1/2}\frac{b\phi}{n}\quad\text{and}

(C.53)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\hat{A}(x^{(j)})\right|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\lesssim q^{1/2}\frac{b\phi}{n}

respectively. In turn, to bound the term (
C.51
), write

\overline{J}_{j}(D_{\mathsf{s}})=\mathbb{E}\left[J(x^{(j)};D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})\mid D_{\mathsf{s}}\right]\penalty\ .

Observe that
\overline{J}_{j}(\cdot)
is symmetric, mean-zero, and bounded by a constant multiple of
\phi
. Moreover,

\overline{A}(x^{(j)})=U_{n,b}(\overline{J}_{j})\penalty\ .

Let
\overline{J}_{j}^{(\ell)}
denote the
\ell
-th Hoeffding projection of
\overline{J}_{j}
. By Part (ii) of
Lemma

2.1
, we have

(C.54)

\displaystyle\overline{A}(x^{(j)})

\displaystyle=\frac{b}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overline{J}_{j}^{(1)}(D_{i})+\sum_{\ell=2}^{b}{b\choose\ell}U_{n,\ell}(\overline{J}_{j}^{(\ell)})\penalty\ .

We bound the two terms in (
C.54
) in succession. By Hoeffding orthogonality,

b\,\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\overline{J}_{j}^{(1)}(D_{i})\right)^{2}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{J}_{j}(D_{[b]})^{2}\right]\lesssim\phi^{2}\penalty\ .

Moreover,
|\overline{J}_{j}^{(1)}(D_{i})|\lesssim\phi
almost surely. Therefore,
Lemma

B.4
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{b}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overline{J}_{j}^{(1)}(D_{i})\right|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\lesssim b\left(\sqrt{\frac{q}{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\overline{J}_{j}^{(1)}(D_{i})\right)^{2}\right]}+\frac{q}{n}\left\|\max_{i\in[n]}\left|\overline{J}_{j}^{(1)}(D_{i})\right|\right\|_{q}\right)

(C.55)

\displaystyle\lesssim\phi\sqrt{\frac{bq}{n}}+\phi\frac{bq}{n}\lesssim q\phi\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\penalty\ .

Next,
Lemma

C.1
and Minkowski’s inequality imply that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\sum_{\ell=2}^{b}{b\choose\ell}U_{n,\ell}(\overline{J}_{j}^{(\ell)})\right|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\ell=2}^{b}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|{b\choose\ell}U_{n,\ell}(\overline{J}_{j}^{(\ell)})\right|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

(C.56)

\displaystyle\lesssim\phi q\sum_{\ell=2}^{b}\left(\frac{Cqb}{n}\right)^{\ell/2}\lesssim\phi q^{2}\frac{b}{n}\penalty\ .

where the final inequality follows from the geometric series formula and the fact that
bq/n\leq c
, which is implied by (
C.16
). Since
bq^{2}/n\leq c
is also implied by (
C.16
), the right-hand side of (
C.56
) is bounded by
q\phi\sqrt{b/n}
. Combining (
C.54
), (
C.55
), and (
C.56
), we obtain

(C.57)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\overline{A}(x^{(j)})\right|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\lesssim q\phi\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\penalty\ .

Thus, the bounds (
C.52
), (
C.53
), and (
C.57
) imply that

(C.58)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\overline{M}_{n}^{(1)}(x^{(j)};g_{0})\right|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\lesssim q\phi\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\penalty\ .

Multiplying both sides of (
C.58
) by

\left(\frac{n}{b^{2}\underline{\sigma}_{b}^{2}}\right)^{1/4}

gives

(C.59)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{n}{b^{2}\underline{\sigma}_{b}^{2}}\right)^{1/4}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\overline{M}_{n}^{(1)}(x^{(j)};g_{0})\right|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\lesssim q\phi\frac{1}{\underline{\sigma}_{b}^{1/2}n^{1/4}}=q\left(\frac{\phi^{4}}{\underline{\sigma}_{b}^{2}n}\right)^{1/4}\penalty\ ,

as required.

C.3.3.
Part (iii)

We give the details of the proof of the stated moment bound on the discrepancy

(C.60)

\overline{M}_{n}(x^{(j)};\theta_{0}(x^{(j)}),\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{M}_{n}(x^{(j)};\theta_{0}(x^{(j)}),g_{0})

only. The argument giving the analogous bound associated with the term

\overline{M}^{(1)}_{n}(x^{(j)};\cdot)
is analogous. Define the functions

(C.61)

\displaystyle F(x;D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}},g)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}f(x,X_{i};D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}},g)\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{F}(x;D_{\mathsf{s}},g)=\mathbb{E}_{\xi_{\mathsf{s}}}\left[F(x;D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}},g)\right]\penalty\ ,

where

\displaystyle f(x,X_{i};D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}},g)

\displaystyle=\kappa(x,X_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})\left(m(D_{i};\theta_{0},g)-m(D_{i};\theta_{0},g_{0})\right)

(C.62)

\displaystyle\quad-\mathbb{E}\left[\kappa(x,X_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})\left(m(D_{i};\theta_{0},g)-m(D_{i};\theta_{0},g_{0})\right)\right]

and the notation
\mathbb{E}_{A}[\cdot]
indicates that we are evaluating the expectation over the randomness in
A
. Define the quantities

(C.63)

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{n}(x;g)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{r}\sum_{q=1}^{r}\left(F(x;D_{\mathsf{s}_{q}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}_{q}},g)-\mathbb{E}_{\mathsf{s}}\left[F(x;D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}},g)\mid\mathbf{D}_{n},\bm{\xi}\right]\right)\penalty\ ,

(C.64)

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{n}(x;g)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{b}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}\left(F(x;D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}},g)-\overline{F}(x;D_{\mathsf{s}},g)\right)\penalty\ ,\quad\text{and}

(C.65)

\displaystyle\overline{F}_{n}(x;g)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{b}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}\overline{F}(x;D_{\mathsf{s}},g)\penalty\ ,

and consider the decomposition

(C.66)

\displaystyle\overline{M}_{n}(x;\theta_{0}(x),g)-\overline{M}_{n}(x;\theta_{0}(x),g_{0})=\tilde{F}_{n}(x;g)+\hat{F}_{n}(x;g)+\overline{F}_{n}(x;g)\penalty\ .

We again apply
Lemma

C.3
to bound (
C.63
) and (
C.64
). In particular, by
Assumption

3.2
, the normalization (
C.19
), and the restriction
n\leq b\sqrt{r}
,
Lemma

C.3
implies that

(C.67)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\tilde{F}_{n}(x^{(j)};\hat{g}_{n})\right|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\lesssim q^{1/2}\frac{b(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\phi}{n}\quad\text{and}

(C.68)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\hat{F}_{n}(x^{(j)};\hat{g}_{n})\right|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\lesssim q^{1/2}\frac{b(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\phi}{n}\penalty\ .

Here, we have used the statistical independence of
\hat{g}_{n}
and
\mathbf{D}_{n}
, together with the fact that the preceding bounds hold uniformly over
g\in\mathcal{G}
.

To bound the term (
C.65
), first observe that

(C.69)

\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{F}(x;D_{\mathsf{s}},g)\right]=0\quad\text{and}\quad\left|\overline{F}(x;D_{\mathsf{s}},g)\right|\lesssim(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\phi

by the boundedness part of
Assumption

3.2
. In turn, observe that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\overline{F}(x;D_{\mathsf{s}},g)\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}\kappa(x,X_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})\mathbb{E}\left[\left(m(D_{i};\theta_{0},g)-m(D_{i};\theta_{0},g_{0})\right)^{2}\mid X_{i}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}\kappa(x,X_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})V(x,g)\right]+\varepsilon_{b}

(C.70)

\displaystyle\lesssim\|g-g_{0}\|_{2,\infty}^{2}+\varepsilon_{b}\penalty\ ,

where the first inequality follows from Honesty and Jensen’s inequality, the second inequality follows from
Assumption

3.3
, Part (ii), and the definition of the shrinkage rate
\varepsilon_{b}
, and the third inequality follows from
Assumption

3.3
, Part (ii), and the normalization that
\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}\kappa(x,X_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi)=1
almost surely.

Let
\overline{F}^{(\ell)}(x;\cdot,g)
denote the
\ell
-th Hoeffding projection of the kernel
\overline{F}(x;\cdot,g)
. By Part (ii) of
Lemma

2.1
, we have

(C.71)

\displaystyle\overline{F}_{n}(x;g)

\displaystyle=\frac{b}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overline{F}^{(1)}(x;D_{i},g)+\sum_{\ell=2}^{b}{b\choose\ell}U_{n,\ell}\left(\overline{F}^{(\ell)}(x;\cdot,g)\right)\penalty\ .

By Hoeffding orthogonality and (
C.70
),

(C.72)

\displaystyle b\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\overline{F}^{(1)}(x;D_{i},g)\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\overline{F}(x;D_{\mathsf{s}},g)\right)^{2}\right]\lesssim\|g-g_{0}\|_{2,\infty}^{2}+\varepsilon_{b}\penalty\ .

Moreover,

\left|\overline{F}^{(1)}(x;D_{i},g)\right|\lesssim(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\phi

almost surely. Hence,
Lemma

B.4
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{b}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overline{F}^{(1)}(x^{(j)};D_{i},g)\right|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

(C.73)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{bq}{n}}\left(\|g-g_{0}\|_{2,\infty}+\varepsilon_{b}^{1/2}\right)+\frac{b}{n}(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\phi q\penalty\ .

Next,
Lemma

C.1
and Minkowski’s inequality imply that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\sum_{\ell=2}^{b}{b\choose\ell}U_{n,\ell}\left(\overline{F}^{(\ell)}(x^{(j)};\cdot,g)\right)\right|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\leq\sum_{\ell=2}^{b}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|{b\choose\ell}U_{n,\ell}\left(\overline{F}^{(\ell)}(x^{(j)};\cdot,g)\right)\right|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

(C.74)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\lesssim(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\phi q\sum_{\ell=2}^{b}\left(\frac{Cqb}{n}\right)^{\ell/2}\lesssim(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\phi q^{2}\frac{b}{n}\penalty\ ,

where the final inequality follows from the geometric series formula and (
C.19
). Combining (
C.71
), (
C.73
), and (
C.74
), we find that, for each fixed
g\in\mathcal{G}
,

(C.75)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\overline{F}_{n}(x^{(j)};g)\right|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{bq}{n}}\left(\|g-g_{0}\|_{2,\infty}+\varepsilon_{b}^{1/2}\right)+\frac{b}{n}(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\phi q^{2}\penalty\ .

Consequently, by the elementary bound

\sqrt{\frac{bq}{n}}\varepsilon_{b}^{1/2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\frac{bq}{n}+\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{b}

and the fact that
\hat{g}_{n}
is statistically independent of
\mathbf{D}_{n}
, the rate condition on
\hat{g}_{n}
gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\overline{F}_{n}(x^{(j)};\hat{g}_{n})\right|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\lesssim q^{3/2}\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}+\sqrt{\frac{bq}{n}}\varepsilon_{b}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\frac{b}{n}(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\phi q^{2}

(C.76)

\displaystyle\lesssim q^{3/2}\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}+\varepsilon_{b}+\frac{b}{n}(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\phi q^{2}\penalty\ .

Putting the pieces together, the decomposition (
C.66
) and the bounds (
C.67
), (
C.68
), and (
C.76
) imply that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\overline{M}_{n}(x^{(j)};\theta_{0}(x^{(j)}),\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{M}_{n}(x^{(j)};\theta_{0}(x^{(j)}),g_{0})\right|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

(C.77)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\lesssim q^{3/2}\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}+\varepsilon_{b}+\frac{b}{n}(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\phi q^{2}\penalty\ ,

where we have absorbed the bounds (
C.67
) and (
C.68
) into the final term. By the normalization (
C.19
), we have

q^{1/2}\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\lesssim 1\quad\text{and}\quad q(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\phi\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\lesssim 1\penalty\ .

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\overline{M}_{n}(x^{(j)};\theta_{0}(x^{(j)}),\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{M}_{n}(x^{(j)};\theta_{0}(x^{(j)}),g_{0})\right|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

(C.78)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\lesssim q\bigg(\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}+\varepsilon_{b}+(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\phi\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\bigg)\penalty\ .

Multiplying both sides by

\sqrt{\frac{n}{b^{2}\underline{\sigma}_{b}^{2}}}

gives (
C.17
).

C.3.4.
Part (iv)

Recall the decomposition

(C.79)

\displaystyle M^{(1)}(x;\theta_{0}(x),g_{0})(\hat{\theta}_{n}(x)-\theta_{0}(x))

(C.80)

\displaystyle=-\overline{M}_{n}(x;\theta_{0}(x),g_{0})

(C.81)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathsf{Bias}_{n}(x;\hat{\theta}_{n}(x),\hat{g}_{n})+\mathsf{Nuis}(x;\theta_{0}(x),\hat{g}_{n})

(C.82)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathsf{Stoch}^{(1)}(x;\hat{\theta}_{n}(x),\hat{g}_{n})+\mathsf{Stoch}^{(2)}(x;\hat{g}_{n})

(C.83)

\displaystyle\quad-(\hat{\theta}_{n}(x)-\theta_{0}(x))\left(M^{(1)}(x;\theta_{0}(x),\hat{g}_{n})-M^{(1)}(x;\theta_{0}(x),g_{0})\right)\penalty\

stated as (
B.68
) in the Proof of
Theorem

B.2
, Part (i), where we note that the various terms appearing in (
C.79
) are defined in (
B.69
) through (
B.72
). Here, we have used the fact that the Hessian
H(x;\theta,g)=0
almost surely, by
Assumption

3.2
. We also use Part (iv) of
Assumption

3.3
, which implies that
\hat{\theta}_{n}(x)
and
\theta_{0}(x)
are uniformly bounded.

First, observe that, by an argument identical to the argument used to establish Part (ii) of this Lemma, i.e., (
C.58
), we have that

(C.84)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\big|\overline{M}_{n}(x^{(j)};\theta_{0}(x^{(j)}),g_{0})\big|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\lesssim q(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\phi\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\penalty\ .

Moreover, as before, Parts (iii) and (iv) of
Assumption

3.3
imply that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|(\hat{\theta}_{n}(x^{(j)})-\theta_{0}(x^{(j)}))\left(M^{(1)}(x^{(j)};\theta_{0}(x^{(j)}),\hat{g}_{n})-M^{(1)}(x^{(j)};\theta_{0}(x^{(j)}),g_{0})\right)\bigg|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

(C.85)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\|\hat{g}_{n}-g_{0}\|_{2,\infty}^{q}\right]^{1/q}\lesssim q\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}\penalty\ .

In turn, observe that Part (i) of this Lemma and Part (iv) of
Assumption

3.3
give that

(C.86)

\displaystyle\left|\mathsf{Bias}_{n}(x^{(j)};\hat{\theta}_{n}(x^{(j)}),\hat{g}_{n})\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\varepsilon_{b}\penalty\ .

and that Neyman orthogonality gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\mathsf{Nuis}(x^{(j)};\theta_{0}(x^{(j)}),\hat{g}_{n})\right|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\|\hat{g}_{n}-g_{0}\|_{2,\infty}^{2q}\right]^{1/q}

(C.87)

\displaystyle\lesssim q^{2}\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\delta_{n,g}^{2}\lesssim q\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}\penalty\ ,

where the final inequality follows from the normalization
q(b/n)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}\leq 1
.

Moreover, as before,
Assumption

B.2
and a Taylor expansion gives

\displaystyle|\mathsf{Stoch}^{(1)}(x;\hat{\theta}_{n}(x),\hat{g}_{n})|

\displaystyle=|(\hat{\theta}_{n}(x)-\theta_{0}(x))\overline{M}_{n}^{(1)}(x;\theta_{0}(x),\hat{g}_{n})|

\displaystyle\leq|\hat{\theta}_{n}(x)-\theta_{0}(x)||\overline{M}_{n}^{(1)}(x;\theta_{0}(x),g_{0})|

(C.88)

\displaystyle\quad+|\hat{\theta}_{n}(x)-\theta_{0}(x)||\overline{M}_{n}^{(1)}(x;\theta_{0}(x),\hat{g}_{n})-\overline{M}_{n}^{(1)}(x;\theta_{0}(x),g_{0})|\penalty\ ,

where we have used the fact that the empirical Hessian
\overline{H}_{n}(x;\tilde{\theta}_{0}(x),\hat{g}_{n})=0
almost surely, by
Assumption

3.2
. Consequently, Part (iv) of
Assumption

3.3
, Part (ii) of this Lemma, and Part (iii) of this Lemma imply that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\mathsf{Stoch}^{(1)}(x^{(j)};\hat{\theta}_{n}(x^{(j)}),\hat{g}_{n})\right|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\lesssim q(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\phi\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+q^{3/2}\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}+\varepsilon_{b}+\frac{b}{n}(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\phi q^{2}

(C.89)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\lesssim q(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\phi\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}+q\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}+q(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\varepsilon_{b}\penalty\ .

Here, the final inequality follows from the normalizations (
C.21
). Similarly, Part (iii) of this Lemma gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\mathsf{Stoch}^{(2)}(x^{(j)};\hat{g}_{n})\right|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\lesssim q^{3/2}\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}+\varepsilon_{b}+\frac{b}{n}(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\phi q^{2}

(C.90)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\lesssim q\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}+q(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\varepsilon_{b}+q(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\phi\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\penalty\ .

Again, the final inequality follows from the normalization (
C.21
).

Consequently, plugging the bounds (
C.84
), (
C.85
), (
C.86
), (
C.87
), (
C.89
), and (
C.90
) into the decomposition (
C.79
), applying Minkowski’s inequality, and using the fact that
M^{(1)}(x;\theta_{0}(x),g_{0})
is bounded from below by Part (iii) of
Assumption

3.3
, we find that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\big|\hat{\theta}_{n}(x^{(j)})-\theta_{0}(x^{(j)})\big|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

(C.91)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\lesssim q\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}+q(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\varepsilon_{b}+q(1+\|\theta_{0}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\|_{\infty})\phi\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\penalty\ .

Multiplying both sides of (
C.91
) by

\left(\frac{n}{b^{2}\underline{\sigma}_{b}^{2}}\right)^{1/4}

gives

(C.92)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{n}{b^{2}\underline{\sigma}_{b}^{2}}\right)^{1/4}\mathbb{E}\left[\big|\hat{\theta}_{n}(x^{(j)})-\theta_{0}(x^{(j)})\big|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\lesssim q\delta_{n,\theta}\penalty\ ,

as required.

C.4.
Proof of
Lemma

C.3

Throughout, we use the shorthand
N_{b}={n\choose b}
. We begin by considering the quantity

(C.93)

\frac{1}{N_{b}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}Z_{\mathsf{s}},\quad\text{where}\quad Z_{\mathsf{s}}=u(D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})-\mathbb{E}\left[u(D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})\mid\mathbf{D}_{\mathsf{s}}\right]\penalty\ .

Conditioned on the data
\mathbf{D}_{n}
, the observations
Z_{\mathsf{s}}
,
\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}
, are centered and mutually independent. Moreover, each component of
Z_{\mathsf{s}}
is bounded by
2\phi
almost surely. Hence, by
Lemma

B.4
, we obtain

(C.94)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\frac{1}{N_{b}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}Z_{\mathsf{s},j}\bigg|^{q}\mid\mathbf{D}_{n}\right]^{1/q}

\displaystyle\lesssim\phi\left(\sqrt{\frac{q}{N_{b}}}+\frac{q}{N_{b}}\right)\penalty\ .

As
N_{b}^{-1/2}\lesssim b/n
for
b>2
, we have that

(C.95)

\displaystyle\phi\left(\sqrt{\frac{q}{N_{b}}}+\frac{q}{N_{b}}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{b\phi q^{1/2}}{n}+\phi q\frac{b^{2}}{n^{2}}\lesssim\frac{b\phi q^{1/2}}{n}\penalty\ ,

where the last inequality follows from the normalization
\phi bq^{1/2}/n\leq 1
and the fact that
\phi\geq 1
. Taking expectations over
\mathbf{D}_{n}
verifies (
C.40
).

Next, consider the quantity

(C.96)

\frac{1}{r}\sum_{q=1}^{r}Z_{q},\quad\text{where}\quad Z_{q}=u(D_{\mathsf{s}_{q}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}_{q}})-\mathbb{E}\left[u(D_{\mathsf{s}_{q}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}_{q}})\mid\mathbf{D}_{n},\bm{\xi}\right]\penalty\ .

Conditioned on
\mathbf{D}_{n}
and
\bm{\xi}
, the observations
Z_{q}
,
q\in[r]
, are centered and mutually independent. Moreover, each component of
Z_{q}
is bounded by
2\phi
almost surely. Thus, the same argument used above gives

(C.97)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\frac{1}{r}\sum_{q=1}^{r}Z_{q}\bigg|^{q}\mid\mathbf{D}_{n},\bm{\xi}\right]^{1/q}\lesssim\phi\left(\sqrt{\frac{q}{r}}+\frac{q}{r}\right)\penalty\ .

Now, the restriction
n\leq\sqrt{r}b
implies that

(C.98)

\displaystyle\phi\left(\sqrt{\frac{q}{r}}+\frac{q}{r}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{b\phi q^{1/2}}{n}+\phi q\frac{b^{2}}{n^{2}}\lesssim\frac{b\phi q^{1/2}}{n}\penalty\ ,

where the final inequality again follows from the normalization
\phi bq^{1/2}/n\leq 1
and the fact that
\phi\geq 1
. Taking expectations over
\mathbf{D}_{n}
and
\bm{\xi}
verifies (
C.41
).

Appendix D
Additional Results and Discussion

D.1.
Stochastic Equicontinuity without Sample Splitting

In the main text, we assume that the nuisance parameter estimator
\hat{g}_{n}
is computed on a separate sample, independent of the data
\mathbf{D}_{n}
. In this section, we give additional conditions under which the nuisance parameter estimator
\hat{g}_{n}
can be computed on the same data used to construct the estimator
\hat{\theta}_{n}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
. Our analysis builds on a similar result given in
Chen et al., (2022)
, who give a pointwise analysis of unconditional moment estimators. By contrast, we give a high-dimensional analysis of conditional moment estimators constructed with subsampled kernels.

In the proof of
Theorem

3.1
, the statistical independence of the estimator
\hat{g}_{n}
and the data
\mathbf{D}_{n}
is only needed in the proof of
Lemma

C.2
, Part (iii). Thus, our objective is to prove an analogous result, without imposing this restriction. In particular, consider the functions

\displaystyle a(x;D_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}},g)

\displaystyle=\kappa(x,X_{i};D_{\mathsf{s}})m(D_{i},g)\quad\text{and}\quad A(x;g)=\mathbb{E}\left[\kappa(x,X_{i};D_{\mathsf{s}})m(D_{i},g)\right],

where
\kappa(\cdot,\cdot;D_{\mathsf{s}})
is a kernel function and

m(D_{i},g)
is a moment function. We are interested in the complete,
deterministic,
U
-statistic

(D.1)

\displaystyle{n\choose b}^{-1}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}F(\bm{x}^{(d)};D_{\mathsf{s}},\hat{g}_{n})

where the kernel function
F(x;D_{\mathsf{s}},g)
is given by

\displaystyle F(x;D_{\mathsf{s}},g)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}f(x;D_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}},g),

\displaystyle f(x;D_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}},g)

\displaystyle=a(x;D_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}},g)-a(x;D_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}},g_{0})-\left(A(x;g)-\mathbb{E}\left[A(x;g_{0})\right]\right),

and
\hat{g}_{n}
is some estimator of the nuisance parameter
g_{0}

computed with the data
\mathbf{D}_{n}
. That is, for the sake of
simplicity, we have restricted our attention to complete, deterministic
subsampled kernel estimators. Incomplete, random, subsampled
kernel estimators can be accommodated through the same methods applied
repeatedly throughout the arguments supporting
Theorem

3.1
.

D.1.1.
Regularity and Stability

To state our additional conditions, we require some additional notation. Let
\mathbf{D}_{n}^{\prime}=(D_{i}^{\prime})_{i=1}^{n}
denote an
independent copy of
\mathbf{D}_{n}
. Let
\hat{g}_{n}^{(-\mathsf{s})}

denote a version of the estimator
\hat{g}_{n}
formed with all observations
in
\mathbf{D}_{n}
, except that the observations
D_{\mathsf{s}}

are replaced by the observations
D_{\mathsf{s}}^{\prime}
. Define the norm

\|g-\tilde{g}\|_{2q,\infty}=\max_{k\in[h]}\max_{j\in[d]}\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\left(g^{(k)}(Z_{i})-\tilde{g}^{(k)}(Z_{i})\right)^{2q}\mid X_{i}=x^{(j)}\right]\right)^{1/2q}

for any nuisance parameters
g=(g^{(k)})_{k=1}^{h}
and
\tilde{g}=(\tilde{g}^{(k)})_{k=1}^{h}

in the space
\mathcal{G}
and any positive integer
q>0
.

First, we require that moment
m(D_{i},g)
satisfies a pair of smoothness conditions.

Assumption D.1
.

Define the higher-order
variogram

V^{(q)}(x;g,g^{\prime})=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(m(D_{i},g)-m(D_{i},g^{\prime})\right)^{2q}\mid X_{i}=x\right]

for each
g
and
g^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{G}
. For each integer

q>0
, the Lipschitz condition

(D.2)

|V^{(q)}(x;g,g^{\prime})-V^{(q)}(x^{\prime};g,g^{\prime})|\lesssim\|x-x^{\prime}\|_{\infty}^{q}

holds for each
x
and
x^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{X}
and the mean-square
continuity condition

(D.3)

\sup_{j\in[d]}V^{(q)}(x^{(j)};g,g^{\prime})\lesssim\|g-g^{\prime}\|_{2q,\infty}^{2q}

holds for each
g
and
g^{\prime}
in
\mathcal{G}
.

Assumption

D.1
is a higher-order analogue to Part (ii) of
Assumption

3.3
.

Second, we impose the following higher-order shrinkage condition.

Assumption D.2
.

Fix a sequence
\varepsilon_{b}
. The kernel
\kappa(\cdot,\cdot,D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})
satisfies the higher-order shrinkage bound

(D.4)

\sup_{P\in\mathbf{P}}\sup_{j\in[d]}\mathbb{E}\left[\max\left\{\|X_{i}-x^{(j)}\|_{\infty}:\kappa(x^{(j)},X_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}},\xi_{\mathsf{s}})>0\right\}^{q}\right]^{1/q}\leq\varepsilon_{b}\penalty\ .

each integer
q\geq 2\vee 2\log(nd)
.

Third, we impose the following higher-order moment stability conditions.

Assumption D.3
.

There exists some increasing sequence

\gamma(q)
, which may depend on the parameters
n
,
b
, and
d
,
such that the
L_{q}
-norm stability bounds

(D.5)

\displaystyle\|m(D_{i},\hat{g}_{n})-m(D_{i},\hat{g}_{n}^{(-[b])})\|_{q}

\displaystyle\lesssim q\gamma(q)\frac{b}{n}\quad\text{and}

(D.6)

\displaystyle\|m(D_{1}^{\prime\prime},\hat{g}_{n})-m(D_{1}^{\prime\prime},\hat{g}_{n}^{(-[b])})\|_{q}

\displaystyle\lesssim q\gamma(q)\frac{b}{n}

hold, where
D_{1}^{\prime\prime}
is an independent copy of
D_{1}
.

Assumption

D.3
quantifies the sensitivity of the moment
m(D_{1},\hat{g}_{n})
to re-sampling
b
elements of the data
\mathbf{D}_{n}
. Analogous conditions are studied in
Abou-Moustafa and Szepesvári, (2019)
and
Chen et al., (2022)
. We discuss moments and estimators that satisfy
Assumption

D.3
in
Section

D.1.3
.

D.1.2.
Stochastic Equicontinuity

The following Theorem gives a suitable bound for the
U
-statistic (
D.1
).

Theorem D.1
.

Suppose that the kernel
\kappa(x,X_{i};D_{\mathsf{s}})
satisfies
Assumption

3.1
and
Assumption

D.2
, that the moment function
m(D_{i},g)
satisfies
Assumption

D.1
, and that the nuisance parameter estimator satisfies the higher-order moment bound

(D.7)

\sup_{P\in\mathbf{P}}\mathbb{E}\left[\|\hat{g}_{n}-g_{0}\|_{2q,\infty}^{2q}\right]^{1/(2q)}\lesssim q\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}

for some sequence
\delta_{n,g}
. If
Assumption

D.3
holds, then the bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{1}{N_{b}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}F(x^{(j)};D_{\mathsf{s}},\hat{g}_{n})\right|^{q}\right]^{1/q}

(D.8)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\lesssim q^{3/2}\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\left(\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}+\varepsilon_{b}^{1/2}+b^{1/q}\gamma(2q)\right)

holds uniformly over
P\in\mathbf{P}
, for each
j\in[d]
and each even integer
q\geq 2\vee 2\log(nd)
.

Remark D.1
.

Compare the bound (
D.8
) to the rate given in
Lemma

C.2
, Part (iii). Taking
q=\overline{q}_{n,d}\asymp\log(dn)
and writing

\gamma_{n,b}^{\star}=b^{1/\overline{q}_{n,d}}\gamma(2\overline{q}_{n,d})\penalty\ ,

the bound (
D.8
) gives the same-sample analogue of the stochastic equicontinuity rate

\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\log^{3/2}(dn)\left(\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}+\varepsilon_{b}^{1/2}+\gamma_{n,b}^{\star}\right)\penalty\ .

The first term is comparable to the corresponding term in
Lemma

C.2
, Part (iii), up to the additional
\log^{1/2}(dn)
factor generated by the generalized Efron–Stein inequality. Moreover, the elementary inequality

\sqrt{\frac{b\log(dn)}{n}}\varepsilon_{b}^{1/2}\leq\frac{b\log(dn)}{n}+\varepsilon_{b}

allows the shrinkage term to be compared directly with the
\varepsilon_{b}
term appearing in
Lemma

C.2
, Part (iii), under the same small-order normalizations used in the proof of
Theorem

3.1
. Thus, relative to the sample-split argument, the principal additional contribution is the stability term

\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\log^{3/2}(dn)\gamma_{n,b}^{\star}\penalty\ .

Consequently, under the conditions of
Theorem

D.1
, if this stability contribution is of smaller order than the stochastic equicontinuity rate in
Lemma

C.2
, Part (iii), a result analogous to
Theorem

3.1
will hold without sample splitting. We discuss conditions under which
\gamma_{n,b}^{\star}
decreases in
Section

D.1.3
.
\blacksquare

Remark D.2
.

Theorem

D.1
follows from an argument similar to the argument developed in
Chen et al., (2022)
. There is one important difference.
Chen et al., (2022)
apply a “double-centering trick,” due to
Kumar et al., (2013)
, to get a variance bound. To obtain a high-dimensional bound, with a logarithmic dependence on the dimension
d
, we replace this step with an application of a generalized Efron-Stein inequality, due to
Boucheron et al., (2005)
.
\blacksquare

D.1.3.
Verifying
Theorem

D.1

In this section, we study conditions under which
Assumption

D.3
is satisfied. First, we impose an additional smoothness condition on the
moment function
m(D_{i},g)
. Throughout, we write the nuisance parameter estimator
\hat{g}_{n}
as
\hat{g}_{n}=(\hat{g}_{k,n})_{k=1}^{h}
.

Assumption D.4
.

The inequalities

(D.9)

\|m(D_{i},\hat{g}_{n})-m(D_{i},\hat{g}_{n}^{(-[b])})\|_{2q}\lesssim\max_{k\in[h]}\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{z\in\mathcal{Z}}\left|\hat{g}_{k,n}(z)-\hat{g}_{k,n}^{(-[b])}(z)\right|^{2q}\right]^{1/(2q)}

and

(D.10)

\|m(D_{i}^{\prime\prime},\hat{g}_{n})-m(D_{i}^{\prime\prime},\hat{g}_{n}^{(-[b])})\|_{2q}\lesssim\max_{k\in[h]}\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\hat{g}_{k,n}(Z_{i}^{\prime\prime})-\hat{g}_{k,n}^{(-[b])}(Z_{i}^{\prime\prime})\right|^{2q}\right]^{1/(2q)}

hold for any positive integer
q
.

The bound (
D.9
)
stipulates that the higher-order moments of
m(D_{i},\hat{g}_{n})-m(D_{i},\hat{g}_{n}^{(-[b])})

are smaller than the supremum of the higher-order moments of
\hat{g}_{k,n}(z)-\hat{g}_{k,n}^{(-[b])}(z)

over the space
\mathcal{Z}
. The bound (
D.10
)
is weaker, as we only need to control the distance
\hat{g}_{k,n}(z)-\hat{g}_{k,n}^{(-[b])}(z)

at
z=Z_{i}^{\prime}
.

Bounds of the form specified by
Assumption

D.4
are satisfied by many standard choices of moment function. For example, consider the moment function

(D.11)

m(D_{i},g)=\mu(1,Z_{i})-\mu(0,Z_{i})+\beta(W_{i},Z_{i})(Y_{i}-\mu(W_{i},Z_{i}))

where the nuisance function
g
collects the moment parameters
g=(\mu,\beta)
. Observe that (
D.11
) is analogous to the moment function (
3.4
) used as a running example in the main text.

Lemma D.1
.

If the quantities

(D.12)

|\hat{\beta}_{n}(W_{i},Z_{i})|\quad\text{and}\quad|Y_{i}-\hat{\mu}_{n}(W_{i},Z_{i})|

are bounded almost surely, then it holds that

\displaystyle\|m(D_{i},\hat{g}_{n})-m(D_{i},\hat{g}_{n}^{(-[b])})\|_{2p}

(D.13)

\displaystyle\lesssim\max\bigg\{\max_{w\in\left\{0,1\right\}}\left\{\|\hat{\mu}_{n}(w,Z_{i})-\hat{\mu}_{n}^{(-[b])}(w,Z_{i})\|_{2p}\right\},

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\|\hat{\mu}_{n}(W_{i},Z_{i})-\hat{\mu}_{n}^{(-[b])}(W_{i},Z_{i})\|_{2p},\|\hat{\beta}_{n}(W_{i},Z_{i})-\hat{\beta}_{n}^{(-[b])}(W_{i},Z_{i})\|_{2p}\bigg\},\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle\|m(D_{i}^{\prime\prime},\hat{g}_{n})-m(D_{i}^{\prime\prime},\hat{g}_{n}^{(-[b])})\|_{2p}

(D.14)

\displaystyle\lesssim\max\bigg\{\max_{w\in\left\{0,1\right\}}\left\{\|\hat{\mu}_{n}(w,Z_{i}^{\prime\prime})-\hat{\mu}_{n}^{(-[b])}(w,Z_{i}^{\prime\prime})\|_{2p}\right\},

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\|\hat{\mu}_{n}(W_{i},Z_{i}^{\prime\prime})-\hat{\mu}_{n}^{(-[b])}(w,Z_{i}^{\prime\prime})\|_{2p},\|\hat{\beta}_{n}(W_{i},Z_{i}^{\prime\prime})-\hat{\beta}_{n}^{(-[b])}(W_{i},Z_{i}^{\prime\prime})\|_{2p}\bigg\},

respectively, where
D_{i}^{\prime\prime}
is an independent copy
of
D_{i}
and
Z_{i}^{\prime\prime}
is an independent copy of

Z_{i}
.

Now, we consider the higher-order stability of the nuisance parameter estimator
\hat{g}_{n}(z)
. For the sake of simplicity, we assume that
\hat{g}_{n}(z)
is scalar valued and takes the form of a complete, deterministic,
U
-statistic

(D.15)

\hat{g}_{n}(z)={n\choose b^{\prime}}^{-1}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b^{\prime}}}u_{z}(D_{\mathsf{s}}),

where
u_{z}(D_{\mathsf{s}})
is some deterministic kernel function
of order
b^{\prime}
. Again, let
\hat{g}^{(-[b])}_{n}(z)
be constructed analogously to
\hat{g}_{n}(z)
using the
\mathbf{D}_{n}
, but replacing
D_{[b]}
with an independent copy

D_{[b]}^{\prime}
.

Lemma D.2
.

Suppose that the kernel
function
u_{z}(D_{\mathsf{s}})
satisfies the bound
|u_{z}(D_{\mathsf{s}})|\leq\phi

almost surely for each
z
in
\mathcal{Z}
. If the estimator
\hat{g}_{n}(z)
is given by (
D.15
), then it holds that

(D.16)

\displaystyle\|\hat{g}_{n}(z)-\hat{g}^{(-[b])}_{n}(z)\|_{q}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{b^{\prime}b}}{n}q\phi+\frac{b^{\prime}}{n}q^{2}\phi.

for each
z
in
\mathcal{Z}
and
2\log(dn)\leq q\leq cn/b^{\prime}

Remark D.3
.

Under
Assumption

D.4
,
Lemma

D.2
implies that

(D.17)

\|m(D_{1}^{\prime\prime},\hat{g}_{n})-m(D_{1}^{\prime\prime},\hat{g}_{n}^{(-[b])})\|_{q}\lesssim\frac{\sqrt{b^{\prime}b}}{n}q\phi+\frac{b^{\prime}}{n}q^{2}\phi.

Thus, relative to
Assumption

D.3
, we can take

\gamma(q)\lesssim\phi\left(\sqrt{\frac{b^{\prime}}{b}}+q\frac{b^{\prime}}{b}\right).

Consequently, at the choice
q\asymp\log(dn)
, invoked in
Theorem

D.1
, this sequence is small provided

\frac{b^{\prime}}{b}\to 0\quad\text{and}\quad\log(dn)\frac{b^{\prime}}{b}\to 0\penalty\ .

In particular,
Lemma

D.2
suggests that the stability contribution in (
D.8
) is negligible if
b^{\prime}
is sufficiently small relative to
b
.
\blacksquare

Remark D.4
.

Assumption

D.3
entails two stability bounds, (
D.5
) and (
D.6
). Under
Assumption

D.4
,
Lemma

D.2
quantifies the sequence
\gamma(q)
introduced on the right-hand side of (
D.5
). By the discussion above, if
b^{\prime}=o(b)
, this sequence is sufficiently small for a result analogous to
Lemma

C.2
, Part (iii) to hold. Handling the bound (
D.6
) requires developing a more refined argument. In particular, under
Assumption

D.4
, to bound the right-hand-side of (
D.5
), we must account for the supremum over the space
\mathcal{Z}
within the expectation. Such a generalization should be reasonably straightforward, potentially through a chaining argument. Roughly speaking, we should expect the supremum to contribute a term like
\log(p)
to the stability, where
p
is the dimension of
\mathcal{Z}
. In this case, in settings where
p
is not high-dimensional, this would not generate any issues. This should be contrasted with analogous suprema taken over the parameter space of a decision tree (which are generated if stochastic equicontinuity is controlled with a union bound). Here the dimension of the parameter space is roughly
2^{\text{Depth of Tree}}
, which may be large enough to make a material difference. However, in order to operationalize this intuition, we would need to place further restrictions sufficient for the smoothness of the Hájek projection of
\hat{g}_{n}(z)
. We leave this for future work.
\blacksquare

D.2.
Cross-Fitting

Theorem

3.1
holds under the assumption that the nuisance parameter estimator
\hat{g}_{n}
is computed on a data set that is statistically independent of the data used to construct the confidence region
\hat{\mathcal{C}}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
. This condition can be achieved by splitting the data into two parts, at the cost of reducing statistical precision and introducing randomness independent of the observed data. In
Section

D.1
, we introduce further restrictions that allow the nuisance parameter estimator
\hat{g}_{n}
and the confidence region
\hat{\mathcal{C}}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
to be computed using the same data. However, in practice, these conditions are more stringent, and it may be unclear whether they are satisfied for a given nuisance parameter estimator
\hat{g}_{n}
.

In this section, we detail an alternative procedure for constructing a confidence region similar to the confidence region specified in
Definition

3.1
, whose validity will hold under the same conditions imposed in
Theorem

3.1
.
The procedure is based on a cross-fit estimator. To introduce this estimator, we require some additional notation. Let
\mathcal{R}_{n,k}
denote the set of partitions of
[n]
into
k
equally sized and mutually exclusive subsets. That is, for each
\mathsf{r}=(\mathsf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{s}_{k})
in
\mathcal{R}_{n,k}
, the sets
\mathsf{s}_{1},\ldots,\mathsf{s}_{k}
are mutually exclusive, have union equal to
[n]
, and are each of size
n/k
. Throughout, we set
q=n/k
and let
\tilde{\mathsf{s}}
denote the complement of the set
\mathsf{s}
in
[n]
. Finally, for each subset
\mathsf{s}
of
[n]
, let
\hat{g}_{\mathsf{s}}
denote a version of the nuisance parameter estimator
\hat{g}_{n}
computed with the data
D_{\mathsf{s}}
.

The cross-fit estimator is computed as follows. For each subset
\mathsf{s}
in
\mathcal{S}_{n,q}
, let the estimator
\hat{\theta}_{\mathsf{s}}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
be constructed by solving the empirical conditional moment equality (
3.2
) using the data
D_{\mathsf{s}}
and the nuisance parameter estimator
\hat{g}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}}
. That is, the nuisance parameter estimator is computed on the sample of data whose indices are not in
\mathsf{s}
. The
k
-fold cross-fit estimator is given by

(D.18)

\hat{\theta}_{\mathsf{r}}(\bm{x}^{(d)})=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathsf{r}}\hat{\theta}_{\mathsf{s}}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\penalty\ ,

where
\mathsf{r}
denotes a random element of
\mathcal{R}_{n,k}
.

It is somewhat unclear how to implement the half-sample bootstrap with the estimator
\hat{\theta}_{\mathsf{r}}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
. We propose the following computationally efficient variant. Let
\mathcal{H}(\mathsf{s})
denote the set of half-samples of the set
\mathsf{s}
, i.e., the set of subsets of
\mathsf{s}
that contain exactly half of its elements. We say that the collection
\mathsf{H}=(\mathsf{h}_{l})_{l=1}^{k}
is a half-sample of the partition
\mathsf{r}=(\mathsf{s}_{l})_{l=1}^{k}
if each
\mathsf{h}_{l}
is an element of
\mathcal{H}(\mathsf{s}_{l})
, i.e., if each
\mathsf{h}_{l}
is a half-sample of
\mathsf{s}_{l}
. For each
l
in
[k]
, let the estimator
\hat{\theta}_{\mathsf{h}_{l}}(\bm{x}^{(d)})
be constructed by solving the empirical conditional moment equality (
3.2
) using the data
D_{\mathsf{h}_{l}}
and the nuisance parameter estimator
\hat{g}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}_{l}}
, i.e., using the same nuisance parameter estimates used to construct (
D.18
). The half-sample
k
-fold cross-split estimator is given by

(D.19)

\hat{\theta}_{\mathsf{H}}(\bm{x}^{(d)})=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{\mathsf{h}\in\mathsf{H}}\hat{\theta}_{\mathsf{h}}(\bm{x}^{(d)})\penalty\ .

In this way, once the
k
-fold cross-fit estimator (
D.18
) has been computed, the nuisance parameter estimates
\hat{g}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}_{1}},\ldots,\hat{g}_{\tilde{\mathsf{s}}_{k}}
do not need to be recomputed to construct the half-sample
k
-fold cross-split estimator (
D.19
). Simultaneous confidence intervals can then be constructed analogously to the intervals introduced in
Definition

3.1
by using the half-sample
k
-fold cross-split bootstrap root

(D.20)

R^{*}_{n}(\bm{x}^{(d)})=\hat{\theta}_{\mathsf{H}}(\bm{x}^{(d)})-\hat{\theta}_{\mathsf{r}}(\bm{x}^{(d)})

in place of the half-sample bootstrap root (
3.8
). In other words, when implementing the half-sample bootstrap based on an estimator constructed with
k
-fold cross-fitting, one can avoid recomputing nuisance parameters if the half-samples are “stratified” across the
k
-folds. The error bound given in
Theorem

3.1
will generalize to the confidence region based on the bootstrap root (
D.20
) through a
straightforward argument, so long as
k
is bounded.

D.3.
Data

In this appendix, we document our treatment of the
Banerjee et al., (2015)
data.
15
15

15

The data from
Banerjee et al., (2015)
were acquired from
https://dataverse.harvard.edu/dataset.xhtml?persistentId=doi:10.7910/DVN/NHIXNT
on
September 10, 2021.
The data from the graduation program implemented in Pakistan are considered in
Chen and Ritzwoller, (2023)
. Here, we consider the data from the graduation program implemented in Ghana, as it has a larger sample size.

The data record measurements of many pre-treatment and post-treatment outcomes for 2,606 individuals in the northern region of Ghana. Baseline survey measurements were made prior to the allocation of treatment. A multifaceted treatment was randomly allocated to 632 of the individuals.
Banerjee et al., (2015)
consider data from two endline surveys, made two years and three years after the initial asset transfer, respectively. For the purpose of this paper, we consider only data from the baseline survey, records of the treatment allocation, and measurements from the first endline survey. We omit data from 164 attrited individuals, none of whom were assigned to the treatment.

The covariate vector
Z_{i}
is composed of measurements of 16 pre-treatment outcomes. Four of these outcomes are associated with consumption: total monthly consumption and total monthly consumption on food, non-food, and durable commodities. Each consumption variable is measured in 2014 US dollars. We transform total monthly consumption to logs, base 10. Three of the variables are associated with assets, each of which is an index constructed from survey data measuring total assets, total productive assets, and total household assets. We transform the total assets measurement to logs, base 10. Five of the outcomes are associated with food security. These consist of four binary variables indicating different aspects of food security, e.g. did a child skip a meal, in addition to an index aggregating these measurements.
16
16

16

There are
2
individuals with missing values for the food security index. We impute these values with the median values of the food security index.
The final four variables are associated with finance and income: the total amount of outstanding loans, the total amount of savings, income from agriculture, and total income from business. The covariate vector
X_{i}
collects the total monthly consumption and assets for each individual. The outcome
Y_{i}
measures the total assets two years after the initial asset transfer. Again, we transform these measurements to logs, base 10.

In
Figure

2
and
Figure

3
, we set the subsample proportion
b/n
equal to 0.05. In constructing the nuisance parameter estimate, we set
b/n
= 0.025. We use
r=200
bootstrap replicates throughout. We use 20,000 trees to construct
Figure

2
and 2,000 trees in each bootstrap replicate to construct
Figure

3
and throughout the simulation.

D.4.
Simulation Details

D.4.1.
Calibration

We calibrate a simulation to the
Banerjee et al., (2015)
data using a collection of GANs. This approach to simulation design was proposed by
Athey et al., (2021)
. Roughly speaking, a GAN is a pair of neural networks. The objective of the first network, the generator, is to generate data that looks like the
Banerjee et al., (2015)
data. The objective of the second network, the discriminator, is to discriminate between the real
Banerjee et al., (2015)
data and the data generated by the generator. These networks compete iteratively until convergence. The idea is that, after convergence, the generator is a good proxy for the true process that generated the
Banerjee et al., (2015)
data. We use the “WGAN” package associated with
Athey et al., (2021)
.

To calibrate our simulation, we estimate three GANs. The first GAN estimates the distribution of the covariates
X_{i}
, i.e., baseline consumption and baseline total assets. The second GAN estimates the distribution of
Z_{i}
conditional on
X_{i}
. Recall that
Z_{i}
collects all baseline covariates, other than the covariates in
X_{i}
. The third GAN estimates the distribution of
Y_{i}
conditioned on
Z_{i}
,
X_{i}
, and
W_{i}
. To generate an observation
D_{i}
, we generate
X_{i}
, generate
Z_{i}
conditioned on
X_{i}
, and generate the potential outcomes
Y_{i}(1)
and
Y_{i}(0)
from the estimated distributions of
Y_{i}
conditioned on
Z_{i}
,
X_{i}
, and
W_{i}=1
and
W_{i}=0
, respectively. The treatment indicator
W_{i}
is drawn i.i.d., Bernoulli with the observed probability in the
Banerjee et al., (2015)
data and we set
Y_{i}=Y_{i}(W_{i})
. In this way, we know the true treatment effect
Y_{i}(1)-Y_{i}(0)
for each unit in our simulation. We draw 10 million observations
D_{i}
with this process. In the simulation, datasets of various sizes are sampled from this collection.

We use a related procedure to determine the true CATE
\theta_{0}(x)
at each value
x
in the query-vector
\mathbf{x}^{(d)}
(i.e., the centers of each of the rectangles displayed in
Figure

2
). Specifically, for each
x
in
\mathbf{x}^{(d)}
, we draw 100,000 observations from the distribution of
Z_{i}
conditioned on
X_{i}=x
. We then draw observations
Y_{i}(1)
and
Y_{i}(0)
for each of these replicates, and compute the average of the true treatment effects
Y_{i}(1)-Y_{i}(0)
.
Figure

F.1
displays these pseudo-true values of the CATE
\theta_{0}(x)
. Our simulation design captures many of the same features of the data recovered by GRF, but gives a somewhat smoother picture of the CATE.

Figure

F.2
displays a scatterplot comparing the moments from the
Banerjee et al., (2015)
data to those generated by our calibrated simulation. The distributions match quite closely.
Figure

F.3
compares a scatter plot of the observed values of baseline consumption and baseline assets in the
Banerjee et al., (2015)
data with a heat-map of the distribution of these covariates in our simulation. The limits of the horizontal and vertical axes in this Figure match
Figures

2
and
3
displayed in the main text. Some observations fall outside of the limits of this figure. The quartiles of baseline log consumption are 3.33, 3.76, and 4.20. The quartiles of baseline assets are -0.45, -0.71, and 0.03.

D.4.2.
Additional Results

Figure

F.4
and
Figure

F.5
give versions of
Figure

2
and
Figure

3
, constructed using the
2
-fold cross-split estimator (
D.18
) and the
2
-fold cross-split bootstrap root (
D.20
). The estimates and confidence bounds are quantitatively and qualitatively very similar.

Figure

F.6
displays results for the simulation presented in
Section

3.5
, analogous to
Figure

4
, for the
2
-fold cross-split estimator (
D.18
) and the
2
-fold cross-split bootstrap root (
D.20
). The confidence region is very slightly anti-conservative, so long as
b/n
is decreasing as
n
increases. The measurements of bias and variance exhibit patterns very similar to the patterns displayed in
Figure

4
.

Appendix E
Proofs Supporting
Appendix

D

E.1.
Proof of
Theorem

D.1

Throughout, we let
x
denote an arbitrary element of
\bm{x}^{(d)}
and use the shorthand
N_{b}={n\choose b}
. We are interested in giving a higher-order moment bound for the quantity

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N_{b}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}F(x;D_{\mathsf{s}},\hat{g}_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{b}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}\bigg(a(x;D_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}},\hat{g}_{n})-a(x;D_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}},g_{0})

(E.1)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad-\left(A(x;\hat{g}_{n})-A(x;g_{0})\right)\bigg)\penalty\ .

We begin by decomposing (
E.1
) into three terms that will be easier to handle in isolation. In particular, by the representation (
2.20
) the quantity (
E.1
) can be re-expressed as

(E.2)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N_{b}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}F(x;D_{\mathsf{s}},\hat{g}_{n})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\bigg\lfloor\frac{n}{b}\bigg\rfloor^{-1}\sum_{l=1}^{\lfloor n/b\rfloor}F_{1}(x;\mathsf{s}_{\pi,l})

(E.3)

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\bigg\lfloor\frac{n}{b}\bigg\rfloor^{-1}\sum_{l=1}^{\lfloor n/b\rfloor}F_{2}(x;\mathsf{s}_{\pi,l})

(E.4)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\bigg\lfloor\frac{n}{b}\bigg\rfloor^{-1}\sum_{l=1}^{\lfloor n/b\rfloor}F_{3}(x;\mathsf{s}_{\pi,l})

where

\displaystyle F_{1}(x;\mathsf{s})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}a(x;D_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}},\hat{g}_{n})-a(x;D_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}},\hat{g}_{n}^{(-\mathsf{s})})

\displaystyle F_{2}(x;\mathsf{s})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}A(x;\hat{g}_{n})-A(x;\hat{g}_{n}^{(-\mathsf{s})}),\quad\text{and}

\displaystyle F_{3}(x;\mathsf{s})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}}a(x;D_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}},\hat{g}_{n}^{(-\mathsf{s})})-a(x;D_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}},g_{0})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad-(A(x;\hat{g}_{n}^{(-\mathsf{s})})-A(x;g_{0})),

respectively. We give suitable probability bounds for the terms in
(
E.2
) through (
E.4
).

We begin by considering the term (
E.2
). Fix any integer

q\geq 1
. We show that

(E.5)

\Big\|\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\bigg\lfloor\frac{n}{b}\bigg\rfloor^{-1}\sum_{l=1}^{\lfloor n/b\rfloor}F_{1}(x;\mathsf{s}_{\pi,l})\Big\|_{2q}\lesssim 2qn^{-1}b\gamma(2q)b^{1/2q}\penalty\ .

To this end, observe that

\displaystyle\|\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\bigg\lfloor\frac{n}{b}\bigg\rfloor^{-1}\sum_{l=1}^{\lfloor n/b\rfloor}F_{1}(x;\mathsf{s}_{\pi,l})\|_{2q}

\displaystyle\leq\|\sum_{i=1}^{b}\kappa(x,X_{i};D_{[b]})(m(D_{i},\hat{g}_{n})-m(D_{i},\hat{g}_{n}^{(-[b])}))\|_{2q}

(E.6)

\displaystyle\leq\|\max_{i\in[b]}|m(D_{i},\hat{g}_{n})-m(D_{i},\hat{g}_{n}^{(-[b])})|^{2}\|_{q}^{1/2},

where the first inequality follows from Jensen’s inequality and the
second inequality follows from
Assumption

3.1
, Part
(ii). Lemma 2.2.2 of
Van der Vaart and Wellner, (1996)
, implies that, if
A_{1},\ldots,A_{n}

are any collection of real-valued random variables, then

(E.7)

\|\max_{i\in[n]}|A_{i}|\|_{q}\lesssim n^{1/q}\max_{i\in[n]}\||A_{i}|\|_{q}.

Thus, we have that

\displaystyle\|\max_{i\in[b]}|m(D_{i},\hat{g}_{n})-m(D_{i},\hat{g}_{n}^{(-[b])})|^{2}\|_{q}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq b^{1/(2q)}\|m(D_{i},\hat{g}_{n})-m(D_{i},\hat{g}_{n}^{(-[b])})\|_{2q}.

\displaystyle\lesssim 2qn^{-1}b\gamma(2q)b^{1/2q}

where the second inequality follows from Assumption
D.3
. Hence, the condition (
E.5
) holds.

We now turn to the term (
E.3
). In this case, we show that

(E.8)

\Big\|\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\bigg\lfloor\frac{n}{b}\bigg\rfloor^{-1}\sum_{l=1}^{\lfloor n/b\rfloor}F_{2}(x;\mathsf{s}_{\pi,l})\Big\|_{2q}\lesssim 2qn^{-1}b\gamma(2q)b^{1/2q}\penalty\ .

To this end, let
\mathbf{D}_{n}^{\prime\prime}=(D_{i}^{\prime\prime})_{i=1}^{n}
be another independent copy of
\mathbf{D}_{n}
. Observe that

\displaystyle\Big\|\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\bigg\lfloor\frac{n}{b}\bigg\rfloor^{-1}\sum_{l=1}^{\lfloor n/b\rfloor}F_{2}(x;\mathsf{s}_{\pi,l})\Big\|_{2q}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{b}\left(A(x;\hat{g}_{n})-A(x;\hat{g}_{n}^{(-[b])})\right)\right\|_{2q}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{b}\kappa(x,X_{i}^{\prime\prime};D_{[b]}^{\prime\prime})\left(m(D_{i}^{\prime\prime},\hat{g}_{n})-m(D_{i}^{\prime\prime},\hat{g}_{n}^{(-[b])})\right)\right\|_{2q}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\left\|\max_{i\in[b]}\left|m(D_{i}^{\prime\prime},\hat{g}_{n})-m(D_{i}^{\prime\prime},\hat{g}_{n}^{(-[b])})\right|^{2}\right\|_{q}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\quad\leq b^{1/(2q)}\left\|m(D_{i}^{\prime\prime},\hat{g}_{n})-m(D_{i}^{\prime\prime},\hat{g}_{n}^{(-[b])})\right\|_{2q}

(E.9)

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim 2qn^{-1}b\gamma(2q)b^{1/2q}\penalty\ ,

where the first two inequalities follow from Jensen’s inequality, the third inequality from
Assumption

3.1
, Part (ii), the fourth inequality follows from (
E.7
), and the final inequality follows from the independent-copy stability bound in
Assumption

D.3
. Hence, the condition (
E.8
) holds.

Finally, we consider the term (
E.4
). The argument here is
somewhat more involved. To simplify exposition, let
k=\lfloor n/b\rfloor

and define the sets
\mathsf{s}_{l}=\left\{(l-1)b+1,\ldots,bl\right\}

for each
l
in
1,\ldots,k
. It will suffice to show that

(E.10)

\left\|\frac{1}{k}\sum_{l=1}^{k}F_{3}(x;\mathsf{s}_{l})\right\|_{q}\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{1}{k}}\left(\left(\frac{1}{k}\right)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}+\varepsilon_{b}^{1/2}+b^{1/q}\gamma(q)\right)q^{3/2}

as Jensen’s inequality implies that

(E.11)

\left\|\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{n}}\bigg\lfloor\frac{n}{b}\bigg\rfloor^{-1}\sum_{l=1}^{\lfloor n/b\rfloor}F_{3}(x;\mathsf{s}_{\pi,l})\right\|_{q}\leq\left\|\frac{1}{k}\sum_{l=1}^{k}F_{3}(x;\mathsf{s}_{l})\right\|_{q}\penalty\ .

To establish the bound (
E.10
), we apply the
following generalized Efron-Stein inequality, due to
Boucheron et al., (2005)
.
See also Chapter 15.2 of
Boucheron et al., (2013)
.

Lemma E.1

(
Boucheron et al.,, 2005
, Theorem 2,
)
.

Let
X=(X_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
be
a sequence of independent random variables and let
X^{\prime}=(X_{i}^{\prime})_{i=1}^{n}

denote an independent copy of
X
. Let
X^{(-i)}
be constructed
by taking
X
and replacing
X_{i}
with
X_{i}^{\prime}
. Consider
the random variable
f(X)
, where
f(\cdot)
is any real-valued
function. It holds that

\displaystyle\|f(X)-\mathbb{E}\left[f(X)\right]\|_{q}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{q}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(f(X)-f(X^{(-i)})\right)^{2}\right\|_{q/2}^{1/2}

for any
q\geq 2
.

Before applying
Lemma

E.1
, observe that

(E.12)

\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{1}{k}\sum_{l=1}^{k}F_{3}(x;\mathsf{s}_{l})\right]=0\penalty\ .

Indeed, conditional on
\hat{g}_{n}^{(-\mathsf{s}_{l})}
, the block

D_{\mathsf{s}_{l}}
is independent of
\hat{g}_{n}^{(-\mathsf{s}_{l})}
.
Thus, by Honesty we find,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}_{l}}(a(x;D_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}_{l}},\hat{g}_{n}^{(-\mathsf{s}_{l})})-a(x;D_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}_{l}},g_{0})-

(E.13)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad(A(x;\hat{g}_{n}^{(-\mathsf{s}_{l})})-A(x;g_{0})))\mid|\hat{g}_{n}^{(-\mathsf{s}_{l})}\bigg]=0\penalty\ .

Taking expectations verifies (
E.12
).

To apply
Lemma

E.1
, let

\hat{g}_{n}^{(-\mathsf{s}_{l},-\mathsf{s}_{r})}
denote a version
of the estimator
\hat{g}_{n}
formed with all observations in
\mathbf{D}_{n}
,
except that the observations
D_{\mathsf{s}_{l}}
and
D_{\mathsf{s}_{r}}

are replaced by the observations
D_{\mathsf{s}_{l}}^{\prime}
and

D_{\mathsf{s}_{r}}^{\prime\prime}
, respectively. Additionally,
let
\mathbf{D}_{n}^{\prime\prime\prime}
be an additional, independent,
copy of
\mathbf{D}_{n}
and let
\tilde{g}_{n}^{(-\mathsf{s}_{l})}

denote a version of the estimator
\hat{g}_{n}
formed with all observations
in
\mathbf{D}_{n}
, except that the observations
D_{\mathsf{s}_{l}}

are replaced by the observations
D_{\mathsf{s}_{l}}^{\prime\prime\prime}
.
Let the quantity
F_{3}^{(-r)}(x;\mathsf{s}_{l})
be given by

\displaystyle F_{3}^{(-r)}(x;\mathsf{s}_{l})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}_{l}}\bigg(a(x;D_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}_{l}},\hat{g}_{n}^{(-\mathsf{s}_{l},-\mathsf{s}_{r})})-a(x;D_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}_{l}},g_{0})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad-\left(A(x;\hat{g}_{n}^{(-\mathsf{s}_{l},-\mathsf{s}_{r})})-A(x;g_{0})\right)\bigg)

if
r\neq l
, and by

\displaystyle F_{3}^{(-l)}(x;\mathsf{s}_{l})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}_{l}}\bigg(a(x;D_{i}^{\prime\prime},D_{\mathsf{s}_{l}}^{\prime\prime},\tilde{g}_{n}^{(-\mathsf{s}_{l})})-a(x;D_{i}^{\prime\prime},D_{\mathsf{s}_{l}}^{\prime\prime},g_{0})

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad\quad-\left(A(x;\tilde{g}_{n}^{(-\mathsf{s}_{l})})-A(x;g_{0})\right)\bigg)

otherwise. Combining (
E.12
) with
Lemma

E.1
implies that

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{k}\sum_{l=1}^{k}F_{3}(x;\mathsf{s}_{l})\right\|_{q}

(E.14)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{q}\bigg\|\sum_{l=1}^{k}\left(\frac{1}{k}\left(F_{3}(x;\mathsf{s}_{l})-F_{3}^{(-l)}(x;\mathsf{s}_{l})\right)+\frac{1}{k}\sum_{r\neq l}\left(F_{3}(x;\mathsf{s}_{r})-F_{3}^{(-l)}(x;\mathsf{s}_{r})\right)\right)^{2}\bigg\|_{q/2}^{1/2}\penalty\ .

We obtain a suitable bound for (
E.14
)
through the application of the following Lemma.

Lemma E.2
.

Suppose that the conditions of
Theorem

D.1
hold. Then

\displaystyle\bigg\|\sum_{l=1}^{k}\left(\frac{1}{k}\left(F_{3}(x^{(j)};\mathsf{s}_{l})-F_{3}^{(-l)}(x^{(j)};\mathsf{s}_{l})\right)+\frac{1}{k}\sum_{r\neq l}\left(F_{3}(x^{(j)};\mathsf{s}_{r})-F_{3}^{(-l)}(x^{(j)};\mathsf{s}_{r})\right)\right)^{2}\bigg\|_{q/2}^{1/2}

(E.15)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{\frac{1}{k}}\left(\left(\frac{1}{k}\right)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}+\varepsilon_{b}^{1/2}+b^{1/q}\gamma(q)\right)q

for each
j\in[d]
and each even integer
q
.

Consequently, the bound (
E.14
)
and
Lemma

E.2
imply (
E.10
).

Putting the pieces together, by the decomposition (
E.2
)–(
E.4
), Minkowski’s inequality, and the bounds (
E.5
), (
E.8
), and (
E.10
), we find that

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{N_{b}}\sum_{\mathsf{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{n,b}}F(x;D_{\mathsf{s}},\hat{g}_{n})\right\|_{q}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\lesssim q\frac{b}{n}\gamma(2q)b^{1/(2q)}+q^{3/2}\sqrt{\frac{1}{k}}\left(\left(\frac{1}{k}\right)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}+\varepsilon_{b}^{1/2}+b^{1/q}\gamma(q)\right)

(E.16)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\lesssim q^{3/2}\sqrt{\frac{b}{n}}\left(\left(\frac{b}{n}\right)^{1/4}\delta_{n,g}+\varepsilon_{b}^{1/2}+b^{1/q}\gamma(2q)\right)\penalty\ ,

where the final inequality follows from
k=\lfloor n/b\rfloor
, the monotonicity of
\gamma(\cdot)
, and the fact that
b\leq n
.
\blacksquare

E.2.
Proof of Lemma
E.2

Throughout, we let
x
denote an arbitrary element of
\bm{x}^{(d)}
and use the shorthand
N_{b}={n\choose b}
.
Observe that

(E.17)

\displaystyle\bigg\|\sum_{l=1}^{k}\left(\frac{1}{k}\left(F_{3}(x;\mathsf{s}_{l})-F_{3}^{(-l)}(x^{(j)};\mathsf{s}_{l})\right)+\frac{1}{k}\sum_{r\neq l}\left(F_{3}(x;\mathsf{s}_{r})-F_{3}^{(-l)}(x;\mathsf{s}_{r})\right)\right)^{2}\bigg\|_{q/2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l=1}^{k}\bigg\|\left(\frac{1}{k}\left(F_{3}(x;\mathsf{s}_{l})-F_{3}^{(-l)}(x^{(j)};\mathsf{s}_{l})\right)+\frac{1}{k}\sum_{r\neq l}\left(F_{3}(x;\mathsf{s}_{r})-F_{3}^{(-l)}(x;\mathsf{s}_{r})\right)\right)^{2}\bigg\|_{q/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\bigg\|\sum_{l=1}^{k}\frac{1}{k^{2}}\left(F_{3}(x;\mathsf{s}_{l})-F_{3}^{(-l)}(x^{(j)};\mathsf{s}_{l})\right)^{2}+\frac{1}{k}\sum_{l=1}^{k}\sum_{r\neq l}\left(F_{3}(x;\mathsf{s}_{r})-F_{3}^{(-l)}(x;\mathsf{s}_{r})\right)^{2}\|_{q/2}

(E.18)

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{k}\|F_{3}(x;\mathsf{s}_{l})\|_{q}^{2}

(E.19)

\displaystyle+k\|F_{3}(x;\mathsf{s}_{r})-F_{3}^{(-l)}(x;\mathsf{s}_{r})\|_{q}^{2},

where the first inequality follows from the triangle inequality, the
second inequality follows from Cauchy-Schwarz and Jensen’s inequality,
and the final inequality follows from the triangle inequality.

Consider the term (
E.18
). Observe that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[|F_{3}(x;D_{\mathsf{s}_{1}})|^{q}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}_{1}}\bigg(a(x;D_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}_{1}},\hat{g}_{n}^{(-\mathsf{s}_{1})})-a(x;D_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}_{1}},g_{0})-\left(A(x;\hat{g}_{n}^{(-\mathsf{s}_{1})})-A(x;g_{0})\right)\bigg)\bigg|^{q}\right]

(E.20)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}_{1}}\left(a(x;D_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}_{1}},\hat{g}_{n}^{(-\mathsf{s}_{1})})-a(x;D_{i},D_{\mathsf{s}_{1}},g_{0})\right)\bigg|^{q}\right]

(E.21)

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}_{1}}\left(A(x;\hat{g}_{n}^{(-\mathsf{s}_{1})})-A(x;g_{0})\right)\bigg|^{q}\right]

by the triangle inequality. We begin by bounding the term (
E.20
). Observe that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\bigg|\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}_{1}}\kappa(x,X_{i};D_{\mathsf{s}_{1}})\left(m(D_{i},\hat{g}_{n}^{(-\mathsf{s}_{1})})-m(D_{i},g_{0})\right)\bigg|^{q}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}_{1}}\kappa(x,X_{i};D_{\mathsf{s}_{1}})\left|m(D_{i},\hat{g}_{n}^{(-\mathsf{s}_{1})})-m(D_{i},g_{0})\right|^{q}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{i\in\mathsf{s}_{1}}\kappa(x,X_{i};D_{\mathsf{s}_{1}})\mathbb{E}\left[\left|m(D_{i},\hat{g}_{n}^{(-\mathsf{s}_{1})})-m(D_{i},g_{0})\right|^{q}\mid X_{i},\hat{g}_{n}^{(-\mathsf{s}_{1})}\right]\mid\hat{g}_{n}^{(-\mathsf{s}_{1})}\right]\right]

(E.22)

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\|\hat{g}_{n}^{(-\mathsf{s}_{1})}-g_{0}\|_{q,\infty}^{q}\right]+\varepsilon_{b}^{q/2},
